# Lockheed Martin F-35 Lightning II

Lockheed Martin F-35 Lightning II

American stealth multirole fighter aircraft

"F35" redirects here. For other uses, see [F35 (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/F35_(disambiguation) "F35 (disambiguation)").

**F-35 Lightning II**

The **Lockheed Martin F-35 Lightning II** is an [American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States "United States") family of single-seat, single-engine, [supersonic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supersonic_speed "Supersonic speed") [stealth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stealth_aircraft "Stealth aircraft") [strike fighters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strike_fighter "Strike fighter"). A [multirole combat aircraft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multirole_combat_aircraft "Multirole combat aircraft") designed for [air superiority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_superiority_fighter "Air superiority fighter") and [strike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attack_aircraft "Attack aircraft") missions, it also has [electronic warfare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electronic_warfare "Electronic warfare") and [intelligence, surveillance, and reconnaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intelligence,_surveillance,_and_reconnaissance "Intelligence, surveillance, and reconnaissance") capabilities. [Lockheed Martin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lockheed_Martin "Lockheed Martin") is the prime contractor, with partners [Northrop Grumman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northrop_Grumman "Northrop Grumman") and [BAE Systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BAE_Systems "BAE Systems"). The aircraft has three main variants: the [conventional takeoff and landing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CTOL "CTOL") [F-35A](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/F-35A_Demonstration_Team "F-35A Demonstration Team"), the [short take-off and vertical-landing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/STOVL "STOVL") F-35B, and the [catapult-assisted take-off but arrested recovery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catapult_Assisted_Take-Off_But_Arrested_Recovery "Catapult Assisted Take-Off But Arrested Recovery") F-35C. With 883 aircraft in service as of 2025[\[update\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Lockheed_Martin_F-35_Lightning_II&action=edit), it is the world's fourth-most-numerous military aircraft, and most-numerous stealth aircraft.[&#8202;](#ref-back-5-0)[\[5\]](#refnote-5)

The aircraft descends from the [Lockheed Martin X-35](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lockheed_Martin_X-35 "Lockheed Martin X-35"), which in 2001 beat the [Boeing X-32](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boeing_X-32 "Boeing X-32") to win the [Joint Strike Fighter program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint_Strike_Fighter_program "Joint Strike Fighter program"), to replace the [F-16 Fighting Falcon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Dynamics_F-16_Fighting_Falcon "General Dynamics F-16 Fighting Falcon"), [F/A-18 Hornet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McDonnell_Douglas_F/A-18_Hornet "McDonnell Douglas F/A-18 Hornet"), the [McDonnell Douglas AV-8B Harrier II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harrier_jump_jet "Harrier jump jet"), and others. Development was primarily funded by the United States, with additional funding from select [NATO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NATO "NATO") members and Australia; [Turkey was removed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lockheed_Martin_F-35_Lightning_II_procurement#Turkey "Lockheed Martin F-35 Lightning II procurement") from the program in 2019.[&#8202;](#ref-back-6-0)[\[6\]](#refnote-6)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Dudley05Mar12-0)[\[7\]](#refnote-Dudley05Mar12)[&#8202;](#ref-back-:4-0)[\[8\]](#refnote-:4)As of April2026[\[update\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Lockheed_Martin_F-35_Lightning_II&action=edit), the US operates all three main variants; both F-35As and F-35Bs are operated [by Italy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lockheed_Martin_F-35_Lightning_II_procurement#Italy "Lockheed Martin F-35 Lightning II procurement") and [Japan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lockheed_Martin_F-35_Lightning_II_procurement#Japan "Lockheed Martin F-35 Lightning II procurement"); F-35As are operated by [Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lockheed_Martin_F-35_Lightning_II_procurement#Australia "Lockheed Martin F-35 Lightning II procurement"), [Belgium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lockheed_Martin_F-35_Lightning_II_procurement#Belgium "Lockheed Martin F-35 Lightning II procurement"), [Denmark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lockheed_Martin_F-35_Lightning_II_procurement#Denmark "Lockheed Martin F-35 Lightning II procurement"), [Netherlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lockheed_Martin_F-35_Lightning_II_procurement#Netherlands "Lockheed Martin F-35 Lightning II procurement"), [Norway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lockheed_Martin_F-35_Lightning_II_procurement#Norway "Lockheed Martin F-35 Lightning II procurement"), [Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lockheed_Martin_F-35_Lightning_II_procurement#Poland "Lockheed Martin F-35 Lightning II procurement"), and [South Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lockheed_Martin_F-35_Lightning_II_procurement#South_Korea "Lockheed Martin F-35 Lightning II procurement"); and F-35Bs are operated [by the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lockheed_Martin_F-35_Lightning_II_procurement#United_Kingdom "Lockheed Martin F-35 Lightning II procurement"). [Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_Air_Force "Israeli Air Force") operates its own [F-35I variant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lockheed_Martin_F-35_Lightning_II_Israeli_procurement "Lockheed Martin F-35 Lightning II Israeli procurement"). Six further NATO members, as well as [Switzerland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lockheed_Martin_F-35_Lightning_II_procurement#Switzerland "Lockheed Martin F-35 Lightning II procurement") and [Singapore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lockheed_Martin_F-35_Lightning_II_procurement#Singapore "Lockheed Martin F-35 Lightning II procurement"), have also placed orders, and other countries are considering purchases.

The program has [drawn criticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lockheed_Martin_F-35_Lightning_II_development#Concerns_over_performance_and_safety "Lockheed Martin F-35 Lightning II development") for its unprecedented size, complexity, [ballooning costs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint_Strike_Fighter_program#Cost_overruns "Joint Strike Fighter program"), and delayed deliveries,[&#8202;](#ref-back-F-35_program_to_get_more_expensive-0)[\[9\]](#refnote-F-35_program_to_get_more_expensive)[&#8202;](#ref-back-11-0)[\[N 1\]](#refnote-11) partially caused by [low rate initial production](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Low_rate_initial_production "Low rate initial production") during [its development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lockheed_Martin_F-35_Lightning_II_development "Lockheed Martin F-35 Lightning II development").[&#8202;](#ref-back-12-0)[\[11\]](#refnote-12)[&#8202;](#ref-back-adamciralsky-0)[\[12\]](#refnote-adamciralsky)As of 2024[\[update\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Lockheed_Martin_F-35_Lightning_II&action=edit), the program is expected to cost US$2trillion through 2088,[&#8202;](#ref-back-:12-0)[\[13\]](#refnote-:12) about half of which is due to inflation.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:17-0)[\[14\]](#refnote-:17) The unit flyaway costs of the 296 aircraft in lots 18-19, for which deliveries start in 2026, are $92M for the F-35A, $121.4M for the F-35B, and $110.8M for the F-35C (2026 dollars).[&#8202;](#ref-back-:18-0)[\[15\]](#refnote-:18) Flyaway costs include all the basic marginal costs to produce one aircraft, including the government-furnished jet engines and radars. The [F135](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pratt_&_Whitney_F135 "Pratt & Whitney F135") engine contributes $20.4million to this flyaway cost as of 2025.[\[update\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Lockheed_Martin_F-35_Lightning_II&action=edit)[&#8202;](#ref-back-17-0)[\[16\]](#refnote-17)

The F-35 first flew in 2006; its variants entered US service in 2015 ( [Marine Corps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Marine_Corps "U.S. Marine Corps") F-35B), 2016 ( [Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Air_Force "United States Air Force") F-35A), and 2019 ( [Navy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Navy "United States Navy") F-35C).[&#8202;](#ref-back-USMC_IOC-1)[\[2\]](#refnote-USMC_IOC)[&#8202;](#ref-back-defready_aug16-1)[\[3\]](#refnote-defready_aug16)[&#8202;](#ref-back-:0-1)[\[4\]](#refnote-:0) The F-35 first saw combat in the [2018 Israeli strikes in Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_House_of_Cards "Operation House of Cards"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-first_strike-0)[\[17\]](#refnote-first_strike) the F-35B in 2018 by [US strikes in Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Freedom's_Sentinel "Operation Freedom's Sentinel"), and the F-35C in 2024 in [US strikes in Yemen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Prosperity_Guardian "Operation Prosperity Guardian"). F-35s have since been used in combat by the US in [Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US-led_intervention_in_Iraq_(2014%E2%80%932021) "US-led intervention in Iraq (2014–2021)"), Iran ( [2025](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_United_States_strikes_on_Iranian_nuclear_sites "2025 United States strikes on Iranian nuclear sites") and [2026](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_Iran_war "2026 Iran war")), and [Venezuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_strikes_in_Venezuela "2026 United States strikes in Venezuela"); by Israel in [Gaza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaza_war "Gaza war"), Iraq, Iran ( [2025](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twelve-Day_War "Twelve-Day War") and [2026](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israel%E2%80%93Hezbollah_conflict_(2023%E2%80%93present) "Israel–Hezbollah conflict (2023–present)")), Lebanon, and [Yemen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/29_September_2024_Israeli_attacks_on_Yemen "29 September 2024 Israeli attacks on Yemen"); and by the UK in Iraq and Syria; F-35As [can deliver](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_weapons_delivery "Nuclear weapons delivery") two US [B61 nuclear bombs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/B61_nuclear_bomb "B61 nuclear bomb"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-20-0)[\[N 2\]](#refnote-20) including those [deployed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_sharing "Nuclear sharing") in the [Netherlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Netherlands_and_weapons_of_mass_destruction "Netherlands and weapons of mass destruction") and [the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_weapons_of_the_United_Kingdom#Return_in_2025 "Nuclear weapons of the United Kingdom"). [Belgium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belgium_and_weapons_of_mass_destruction "Belgium and weapons of mass destruction"), [Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Germany_and_weapons_of_mass_destruction#Post-Cold_War "Germany and weapons of mass destruction"), [Italy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_nuclear_weapons_program#Nuclear_weapons_in_Italy_since_1975 "Italian nuclear weapons program"), and the [UK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_weapons_of_the_United_Kingdom#F-35A_procurement "Nuclear weapons of the United Kingdom") plan to buy nuclear-capable F-35As. The US plans to buy 2,456 F-35s through 2044, which will represent the bulk of its crewed fixed-wing tactical aviation for several decades; the aircraft is to be a cornerstone of NATO and US-allied air power and operate until 2088.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FY2020_SAR-0)[\[19\]](#refnote-FY2020_SAR)[&#8202;](#ref-back-22-0)[\[20\]](#refnote-22)[&#8202;](#ref-back-23-0)[\[21\]](#refnote-23)[&#8202;](#ref-back-:12-1)[\[13\]](#refnote-:12)

## Development

Main article: [Lockheed Martin F-35 Lightning II development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lockheed_Martin_F-35_Lightning_II_development "Lockheed Martin F-35 Lightning II development")

### Program origins

The F-35 was the product of the [Joint Strike Fighter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint_Strike_Fighter "Joint Strike Fighter") [(JSF) program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint_Strike_Fighter_program "Joint Strike Fighter program"), which was the merger of various combat aircraft programs from the 1980s and 1990s. One progenitor program was the [Defense Advanced Research Projects Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defense_Advanced_Research_Projects_Agency "Defense Advanced Research Projects Agency") (DARPA) Advanced Short Take-Off/Vertical Landing (ASTOVL), which ran from 1983 to 1994; ASTOVL aimed to develop a [Harrier jump jet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harrier_jump_jet "Harrier jump jet") replacement for the [US Marine Corps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Marine_Corps "United States Marine Corps") (USMC) and the UK [Royal Navy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Navy "Royal Navy"). Under one of ASTOVL's classified programs, the Supersonic STOVL Fighter (SSF), Lockheed's [Skunk Works](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skunk_Works "Skunk Works") conducted research for a stealthy supersonic STOVL fighter intended for both US Air Force (USAF) and USMC; among key [STOVL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/V/STOL "V/STOL") technologies explored was the shaft-driven lift fan (SDLF) system. Lockheed's concept was a single-engine [canard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canard_(aeronautics) "Canard (aeronautics)") delta aircraft weighing about 24,000lb (11,000kg) empty. ASTOVL was rechristened as the [Common Affordable Lightweight Fighter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_Affordable_Lightweight_Fighter "Common Affordable Lightweight Fighter") (CALF) in 1993 and involved [Lockheed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lockheed_Corporation "Lockheed Corporation"), [McDonnell Douglas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McDonnell_Douglas "McDonnell Douglas"), and [Boeing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boeing "Boeing").[&#8202;](#ref-back-24-0)[\[22\]](#refnote-24)[&#8202;](#ref-back-pre-jast_hist-0)[\[23\]](#refnote-pre-jast_hist)

The end of the [Cold War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cold_War "Cold War") and the [collapse of the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collapse_of_the_Soviet_Union "Collapse of the Soviet Union") in 1991 caused considerable reductions in [Department of Defense](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Department_of_Defense "United States Department of Defense") (DoD) spending and subsequent restructuring. In 1993, the Joint Advanced Strike Technology (JAST) program emerged following the cancellation of the USAF's Multi-Role Fighter (MRF) and US Navy's (USN) Advanced Attack/Fighter (A/F-X) programs. MRF, a program for a relatively affordable [F-16 Fighting Falcon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Dynamics_F-16_Fighting_Falcon "General Dynamics F-16 Fighting Falcon") replacement, was scaled back and delayed due to post–Cold War defense posture easing F-16 fleet usage and thus extending its service life as well as increasing budget pressure from the [Lockheed Martin F-22](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lockheed_Martin_F-22_Raptor "Lockheed Martin F-22 Raptor") [Advanced Tactical Fighter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Advanced_Tactical_Fighter "Advanced Tactical Fighter") (ATF) program. The A/F-X, initially known as the Advanced-Attack (A-X), began in 1991 as the USN's follow-on to the Advanced Tactical Aircraft (ATA) program for a [Grumman A-6 Intruder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grumman_A-6_Intruder "Grumman A-6 Intruder") replacement; the ATA's resulting [McDonnell Douglas A-12 Avenger II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McDonnell_Douglas_A-12_Avenger_II "McDonnell Douglas A-12 Avenger II") had been canceled due to technical problems and cost overruns in 1991. In the same year, the termination of the Naval Advanced Tactical Fighter (NATF), a naval development of USAF's ATF program to replace the [Grumman F-14 Tomcat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grumman_F-14_Tomcat "Grumman F-14 Tomcat"), resulted in additional fighter capability being added to A-X, which was then renamed A/F-X. Amid increased budget pressure, the DoD's Bottom-Up Review (BUR) in September 1993 announced MRF's and A/F-X's cancellations, with applicable experience brought to the emerging JAST program.[&#8202;](#ref-back-pre-jast_hist-1)[\[23\]](#refnote-pre-jast_hist) JAST was not meant to develop a new aircraft, but rather to develop requirements, mature technologies, and demonstrate concepts for advanced strike warfare.[&#8202;](#ref-back-jast_hist-0)[\[24\]](#refnote-jast_hist)

As JAST progressed, the need for concept demonstrator aircraft by 1996 emerged, which would coincide with the full-scale flight demonstrator phase of ASTOVL/CALF. Because the ASTOVL/CALF concept appeared to align with the JAST charter, the two programs were eventually merged in 1994 under the JAST name, with the program now serving the USAF, USMC, and USN.[&#8202;](#ref-back-jast_hist-1)[\[24\]](#refnote-jast_hist) JAST was subsequently renamed to Joint Strike Fighter (JSF) in 1995, with STOVL submissions by McDonnell Douglas, [Northrop Grumman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northrop_Grumman "Northrop Grumman"), Lockheed Martin,[&#8202;](#ref-back-27-0)[\[N 3\]](#refnote-27) and Boeing. The JSF was expected to eventually replace large numbers of multi-role and strike fighters in the inventories of the US and its allies, including the [Harrier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harrier_jump_jet "Harrier jump jet"), [F-16](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Dynamics_F-16_Fighting_Falcon "General Dynamics F-16 Fighting Falcon"), [F/A-18](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McDonnell_Douglas_F/A-18_Hornet "McDonnell Douglas F/A-18 Hornet"), [Fairchild A-10 Thunderbolt II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fairchild_Republic_A-10_Thunderbolt_II "Fairchild Republic A-10 Thunderbolt II"), and [Lockheed F-117 Nighthawk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lockheed_F-117_Nighthawk "Lockheed F-117 Nighthawk").[&#8202;](#ref-back-28-0)[\[25\]](#refnote-28)

International participation is a key aspect of the JSF program, starting with United Kingdom participation in the ASTOVL program. Many international partners requiring modernization of their air forces were interested in the JSF. The United Kingdom joined JAST/JSF as a founding member in 1995 and thus became the only Tier 1 partner of the JSF program;[&#8202;](#ref-back-T1partner-0)[\[26\]](#refnote-T1partner) Italy, the Netherlands, Denmark, Norway, Canada, Australia, and Turkey joined the program during the Concept Demonstration Phase (CDP), with Italy and the Netherlands being Tier 2 partners and the rest Tier 3. Consequently, the aircraft was developed in cooperation with international partners and available for export.[&#8202;](#ref-back-UKJAST-0)[\[27\]](#refnote-UKJAST)

### JSF competition

Main articles: [Joint Strike Fighter program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint_Strike_Fighter_program "Joint Strike Fighter program") and [Lockheed Martin X-35](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lockheed_Martin_X-35 "Lockheed Martin X-35")

Boeing and Lockheed Martin were selected in early 1997 for CDP, with their concept demonstrator aircraft designated [X-32](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boeing_X-32 "Boeing X-32") and [X-35](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lockheed_Martin_X-35 "Lockheed Martin X-35"), respectively; the McDonnell Douglas team was eliminated, and Northrop Grumman and [British Aerospace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Aerospace "British Aerospace") joined the Lockheed Martin team. Each firm would produce two prototype air vehicles to demonstrate conventional takeoff and landing (CTOL), carrier takeoff and landing (CV), and STOVL.[&#8202;](#ref-back-31-0)[\[N 4\]](#refnote-31) Lockheed Martin's design would make use of the work on the SDLF system conducted under the ASTOVL/CALF program. The key aspect of the X-35 that enabled STOVL operation, the SDLF system consists of the lift fan in the forward center fuselage that could be activated by engaging a clutch that connects the driveshaft to the turbines and thus augmenting the thrust from the engine's swivel nozzle. Research from prior aircraft incorporating similar systems, such as the [Convair Model 200](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convair_Model_200 "Convair Model 200"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-33-0)[\[N 5\]](#refnote-33) [Rockwell XFV-12](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rockwell_XFV-12 "Rockwell XFV-12"), and [Yakovlev Yak-141](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yakovlev_Yak-141 "Yakovlev Yak-141"), was also taken into consideration.[&#8202;](#ref-back-GovExec-0)[\[29\]](#refnote-GovExec)[&#8202;](#ref-back-LiftFanPatent1-0)[\[30\]](#refnote-LiftFanPatent1)[&#8202;](#ref-back-36-0)[\[31\]](#refnote-36) By contrast, Boeing's X-32 employed a direct lift system that the augmented turbofan would be reconfigured to when engaging in STOVL operation.

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2f/X-35B_Edwards.jpg/500px-X-35B_Edwards.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:X-35B_Edwards.jpg) X-35B flying over Edwards Air Force Base

Lockheed Martin's commonality strategy was to replace the STOVL variant's SDLF with a fuel tank and the aft swivel nozzle with a two-dimensional thrust vectoring nozzle for the CTOL variant.[&#8202;](#ref-back-37-0)[\[N 6\]](#refnote-37) STOVL operation is made possible through a patented shaft-driven LiftFan propulsion system.[&#8202;](#ref-back-38-0)[\[32\]](#refnote-38) This would enable an identical aerodynamic configuration for the STOVL and CTOL variants, while the CV variant would have an enlarged wing to reduce landing speed for carrier recovery. Due to aerodynamic characteristics and carrier recovery requirements from the JAST merger, the design configuration settled on a conventional tail compared to the canard delta design from the ASTOVL/CALF; notably, the conventional tail configuration offers much lower risk for carrier recovery compared to the ASTOVL/CALF canard configuration, which was designed without carrier compatibility in mind. This enabled greater commonality between all three variants, as the commonality goal was important at this design stage.[&#8202;](#ref-back-jast_to_ioc-0)[\[33\]](#refnote-jast_to_ioc) Lockheed Martin's prototypes would consist of the X-35A for demonstrating CTOL before converting it to the X-35B for STOVL demonstration and the larger-winged X-35C for CV compatibility demonstration.[&#8202;](#ref-back-JPP-0)[\[34\]](#refnote-JPP)

The X-35A first flew on 24 October 2000 and conducted flight tests for subsonic and supersonic flying qualities, handling, range, and maneuver performance.[&#8202;](#ref-back-jsf_hist-0)[\[35\]](#refnote-jsf_hist) After 28 flights, the aircraft was then converted into the X-35B for STOVL testing, with key changes including the addition of the SDLF, the three-bearing swivel module (3BSM), and roll-control ducts. The X-35B would successfully demonstrate the SDLF system by performing stable hover, vertical landing, and short takeoff in less than 500ft (150m).[&#8202;](#ref-back-jast_to_ioc-1)[\[33\]](#refnote-jast_to_ioc)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Nova_transcript_X-planes-0)[\[36\]](#refnote-Nova_transcript_X-planes) The X-35C first flew on 16 December 2000 and conducted field landing carrier practice tests.[&#8202;](#ref-back-jsf_hist-1)[\[35\]](#refnote-jsf_hist)

On 26 October 2001, [Lockheed Martin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lockheed_Martin "Lockheed Martin") was declared the winner and was awarded the System Development and Demonstration (SDD) contract; [Pratt & Whitney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pratt_&_Whitney "Pratt & Whitney") was separately awarded a development contract for the F135 engine for the JSF.[&#8202;](#ref-back-f35_acq_hist-0)[\[37\]](#refnote-f35_acq_hist) The F-35 designation, which was out of sequence with [standard DoD numbering](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1962_United_States_Tri-Service_aircraft_designation_system#Design_number "1962 United States Tri-Service aircraft designation system"), was allegedly determined on the spot by program manager Major General Mike Hough; this came as a surprise even to Lockheed Martin, which had expected the F-24 designation for the JSF.[&#8202;](#ref-back-44-0)[\[38\]](#refnote-44)

### Design and production

[![Engineer handling a metallic scale model of jet fighter in wind-tunnel](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f4/F-35_Wind_Tunnel_Model.jpg/500px-F-35_Wind_Tunnel_Model.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:F-35_Wind_Tunnel_Model.jpg) An F-35 [wind tunnel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wind_tunnel "Wind tunnel") testing model in 16-foot (5m) transonic wind tunnel at the [Arnold Engineering Development Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arnold_Engineering_Development_Center "Arnold Engineering Development Center")

As the JSF program moved into the System Development and Demonstration phase, the X-35 demonstrator design was modified to create the F-35 combat aircraft. The forward fuselage was lengthened by 5 inches (13cm) to make room for mission avionics, while the horizontal stabilizers were moved 2 inches (5.1cm) aft to retain balance and control. The [diverterless supersonic inlet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diverterless_supersonic_inlet "Diverterless supersonic inlet") changed from a four-sided to a three-sided cowl shape and was moved 30 inches (76cm) aft. The fuselage section was fuller, the top surface raised by 1 inch (2.5cm) along the centerline and the lower surface bulged to accommodate weapons bays. Following the designation of the X-35 prototypes, the three variants were designated F-35A ( [CTOL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CTOL "CTOL")), F-35B (STOVL), and F-35C (CV), all with a design service life of 8,000 hours. Prime contractor Lockheed Martin performs overall systems integration and final assembly and checkout (FACO) at [Air Force Plant 4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Air_Force_Plant_4 "United States Air Force Plant 4") in [Fort Worth, Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fort_Worth,_Texas "Fort Worth, Texas"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-45-0)[\[N 7\]](#refnote-45) while Northrop Grumman and [BAE Systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BAE_Systems "BAE Systems") supply components for mission systems and airframe.[&#8202;](#ref-back-46-0)[\[39\]](#refnote-46)[&#8202;](#ref-back-47-0)[\[40\]](#refnote-47)

Adding the systems of a fighter aircraft added weight. The F-35B gained the most, largely due to a 2003 decision to enlarge the weapons bays for commonality between variants; the total weight growth was reportedly up to 2,200 pounds (1,000kg), over 8%, causing all STOVL key performance parameter (KPP) thresholds to be missed.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Keijsper_2007,_p._119-0)[\[41\]](#refnote-Keijsper_2007,_p._119) In December 2003, the STOVL Weight Attack Team (SWAT) was formed to reduce the weight increase; changes included thinned airframe members, smaller weapons bays and vertical stabilizers, less thrust fed to the roll-post outlets, and redesigning the wing-mate joint, electrical elements, and the airframe immediately aft of the cockpit. The inlet was also revised to accommodate more powerful, greater mass flow engines.[&#8202;](#ref-back-thrust_target-0)[\[42\]](#refnote-thrust_target)[&#8202;](#ref-back-50-0)[\[43\]](#refnote-50) Many changes from the SWAT effort were applied to all three variants for commonality. By September 2004, these efforts had reduced the F-35B's weight by over 3,000 pounds (1,400kg), while the F-35A and F-35C were reduced in weight by 2,400 pounds (1,100kg) and 1,900 pounds (860kg), respectively.[&#8202;](#ref-back-jast_to_ioc-2)[\[33\]](#refnote-jast_to_ioc)[&#8202;](#ref-back-51-0)[\[44\]](#refnote-51) The weight reduction work cost $6.2billion and caused an 18-month delay.[&#8202;](#ref-back-C4WDefault-0)[\[45\]](#refnote-C4WDefault)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/66/F-35A_-_Inauguration_Towing.jpg/500px-F-35A_-_Inauguration_Towing.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:F-35A_-_Inauguration_Towing.jpg) The first F-35A prototype, AA-1, being towed to its inauguration ceremony on 7 July 2006

The first F-35A, designated AA-1, was rolled out at Fort Worth on 19 February 2006 and first flew on 15 December 2006 with chief test pilot Jon S. Beesley at the controls.[&#8202;](#ref-back-53-0)[\[N 8\]](#refnote-53)[&#8202;](#ref-back-9jul2013-0)[\[46\]](#refnote-9jul2013) In 2006, the F-35 was given the name "Lightning II" in honor of both the [Lockheed P-38 Lightning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lockheed_P-38_Lightning "Lockheed P-38 Lightning") of World War II and the [English Electric Lightning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_Electric_Lightning "English Electric Lightning").[&#8202;](#ref-back-55-0)[\[47\]](#refnote-55)[&#8202;](#ref-back-56-0)[\[48\]](#refnote-56) Some USAF pilots have nicknamed the aircraft "Panther" instead, and other nicknames include "Fat Amy" and "Battle Penguin".[&#8202;](#ref-back-57-0)[\[49\]](#refnote-57)[&#8202;](#ref-back-58-0)[\[50\]](#refnote-58)[&#8202;](#ref-back-59-0)[\[51\]](#refnote-59)

The aircraft's software was developed as six releases, or Blocks, for SDD. The first two Blocks, 1A and 1B, readied the F-35 for initial pilot training and multi-level security. Block 2A improved the training capabilities, while 2B was the first combat-ready release planned for the USMC's [Initial Operating Capability](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Initial_Operating_Capability "Initial Operating Capability") (IOC). Block 3i retains the capabilities of 2B while having new Technology Refresh 2 (TR-2) hardware and was planned for the USAF's IOC. The final release for SDD, Block 3F, would have full flight envelope and all baseline combat capabilities. Alongside software releases, each block also incorporates avionics hardware updates and air vehicle improvements from flight and structural testing.[&#8202;](#ref-back-lm_sw-0)[\[52\]](#refnote-lm_sw) In what is known as "concurrency", some [low rate initial production](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Low_rate_initial_production "Low rate initial production") (LRIP) aircraft lots would be delivered in early Block configurations and eventually upgraded to Block 3F once development is complete.[&#8202;](#ref-back-61-0)[\[53\]](#refnote-61) After 17,000flight test hours, the final flight for the SDD phase was completed in April 2018.[&#8202;](#ref-back-62-0)[\[54\]](#refnote-62) Like the F-22, the F-35 has been targeted by [cyberattacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyberattack "Cyberattack") and technology theft efforts, as well as potential vulnerabilities in the integrity of the supply chain.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:3-0)[\[55\]](#refnote-:3)[&#8202;](#ref-back-64-0)[\[56\]](#refnote-64)[&#8202;](#ref-back-65-0)[\[57\]](#refnote-65)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fb/CF-1_flight_test.jpg/500px-CF-1_flight_test.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:CF-1_flight_test.jpg) The first F-35C flight sciences aircraft, CF-01, conducts a test flight over the [Chesapeake Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chesapeake_Bay "Chesapeake Bay") in February 2011.

Testing found several major problems: early F-35B airframes were vulnerable to premature cracking,[&#8202;](#ref-back-66-0)[\[N 9\]](#refnote-66) the F-35C arrestor hook design was unreliable, fuel tanks were too vulnerable to lightning strikes, the helmet display had problems, and more. Software was repeatedly delayed due to its unprecedented scope and complexity. In 2009, the DoD Joint Estimate Team (JET) estimated that the program was 30 months behind the public schedule.[&#8202;](#ref-back-internalmemo-0)[\[58\]](#refnote-internalmemo)[&#8202;](#ref-back-68-0)[\[59\]](#refnote-68) In 2011, the program was "re-baselined"; that is, its cost and schedule goals were changed, pushing the IOC from the planned 2010 to July 2015.[&#8202;](#ref-back-69-0)[\[60\]](#refnote-69)[&#8202;](#ref-back-70-0)[\[61\]](#refnote-70) The decision to simultaneously test, fix defects, and begin production was criticized as inefficient; in 2014, [Under Secretary of Defense for Acquisition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Under_Secretary_of_Defense_for_Acquisition "Under Secretary of Defense for Acquisition") [Frank Kendall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frank_Kendall_III "Frank Kendall III") called it "acquisition malpractice".[&#8202;](#ref-back-71-0)[\[62\]](#refnote-71) The three variants shared just 25% of their parts, far below the anticipated commonality of 70%.[&#8202;](#ref-back-72-0)[\[63\]](#refnote-72)

The program received considerable criticism for cost overruns and for the total projected lifetime cost, as well as quality management shortcomings by contractors.[&#8202;](#ref-back-violations-0)[\[64\]](#refnote-violations)[&#8202;](#ref-back-barrett-0)[\[65\]](#refnote-barrett)As of August2023[\[update\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Lockheed_Martin_F-35_Lightning_II&action=edit), the program was 80% over budget and 10 years late.[&#8202;](#ref-back-75-0)[\[66\]](#refnote-75)

The JSF program was expected to cost about $200billion for acquisition in base-year 2002 dollars when SDD was awarded in 2001.[&#8202;](#ref-back-76-0)[\[67\]](#refnote-76)[&#8202;](#ref-back-77-0)[\[68\]](#refnote-77) As early as 2005, the [Government Accountability Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_Accountability_Office "Government Accountability Office") (GAO) had identified major program risks in cost and schedule.[&#8202;](#ref-back-78-0)[\[69\]](#refnote-78) The costly delays strained the relationship between the Pentagon and contractors.[&#8202;](#ref-back-79-0)[\[70\]](#refnote-79) By 2017, delays and cost overruns had pushed the F-35 program's expected acquisition costs to $406.5billion, with total lifetime cost through 2070 to $1.5trillion in then-year dollars, which also includes operations and maintenance.[&#8202;](#ref-back-C4WDefault-1)[\[71\]](#refnote-C4WDefault)[&#8202;](#ref-back-81-0)[\[72\]](#refnote-81)[&#8202;](#ref-back-82-0)[\[73\]](#refnote-82) By 2024, the lifetime was extended to 2088, and the total lifetime cost was estimated at $2.1 trillion, about half of which was due to inflation.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:17-1)[\[14\]](#refnote-:17)[&#8202;](#ref-back-:12-2)[\[13\]](#refnote-:12)

The F-35A's unit cost (not including engine) for LRIP Lot 13 was $79.2million in base-year 2012 dollars.[&#8202;](#ref-back-lrip_13-0)[\[74\]](#refnote-lrip_13) Delays in development and operational test and evaluation, including integration into the [Joint Simulation Environment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint_Simulation_Environment "Joint Simulation Environment"), pushed the full-rate production decision from the end of 2019 to March 2024, although actual production rate had already approached the full rate by 2020; the combined full rate at the Fort Worth, Italy, and Japan FACO plants is 156 aircraft annually.[&#8202;](#ref-back-gao-0)[\[75\]](#refnote-gao)[&#8202;](#ref-back-F-35MilestoneC-0)[\[76\]](#refnote-F-35MilestoneC)

### Upgrades and further development

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/71/Sailors_prepare_to_taxi_an_F-35C_Lightning_II_on_aircraft_carrier_USS_Carl_Vinson_%28March_30%2C_2023%29.webp/500px-Sailors_prepare_to_taxi_an_F-35C_Lightning_II_on_aircraft_carrier_USS_Carl_Vinson_%28March_30%2C_2023%29.webp?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Sailors_prepare_to_taxi_an_F-35C_Lightning_II_on_aircraft_carrier_USS_Carl_Vinson_(March_30,_2023).webp) Sailors prepare to taxi an F-35C Lightning II on aircraft carrier [USS Carl Vinson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS_Carl_Vinson "USS Carl Vinson").

The F-35 is expected to be continually upgraded over its lifetime. The first combat-capable Block 2B configuration, which had basic air-to-air and strike capabilities, was declared ready by the USMC in July 2015.[&#8202;](#ref-back-USMC_IOC-2)[\[2\]](#refnote-USMC_IOC) The Block 3F configuration began operational test and evaluation (OT&E) in December 2018, and its completion in late 2023 concluded SDD in March 2024.[&#8202;](#ref-back-86-0)[\[77\]](#refnote-86) The F-35 program is also conducting sustainment and upgrade development, with early aircraft from LRIP lot 2 onwards gradually upgraded to the baseline Block 3F standard by 2021.[&#8202;](#ref-back-87-0)[\[78\]](#refnote-87)\[ _[needs update](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Manual_of_Style/Dates_and_numbers#Chronological_items "Wikipedia:Manual of Style/Dates and numbers")_\]

With Block 3F as the final build for SDD, the first major upgrade program is Block 4, which began development in 2019 and was initially captured under the Continuous Capability Development and Delivery (C2D2) program. Block 4 is expected to enter service in incremental steps from the late 2020s to early 2030s and integrates additional weapons, including those unique to international customers, improved sensor capabilities including the new AN/APG-85 AESA radar and additional ESM bandwidth, and adds Remotely Operated Video Enhanced Receiver ( [ROVER](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ROVER "ROVER")) support.[&#8202;](#ref-back-88-0)[\[79\]](#refnote-88)[&#8202;](#ref-back-89-0)[\[80\]](#refnote-89) C2D2 also places greater emphasis on [agile software development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agile_software_development "Agile software development") to enable quicker releases.[&#8202;](#ref-back-90-0)[\[81\]](#refnote-90)

The key enabler of Block 4 is Technology Refresh 3 (TR-3) avionics hardware, which consists of new display, core processor, and memory modules to support increased processing requirements, as well as an engine upgrade that increases the amount of cooling available to support the additional mission systems. The engine upgrade effort explored both improvements to the F135 as well as significantly more power and efficient [adaptive cycle engines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adaptive_cycle_engine "Adaptive cycle engine"). In 2018, General Electric and Pratt & Whitney were awarded contracts to develop adaptive cycle engines for potential application in the F-35,[&#8202;](#ref-back-91-0)[\[N 10\]](#refnote-91) and in 2022, the F-35 Adaptive Engine Replacement program was launched to integrate them.[&#8202;](#ref-back-fg20180709-0)[\[82\]](#refnote-fg20180709)[&#8202;](#ref-back-faer_rfi-0)[\[83\]](#refnote-faer_rfi) However, in 2023 the USAF chose an improved F135 under the Engine Core Upgrade (ECU) program over an adaptive cycle engine due to cost as well as concerns over risk of integrating the new engine, initially designed for the F-35A, on the B and C.[&#8202;](#ref-back-f35_pw_ecu_win-0)[\[84\]](#refnote-f35_pw_ecu_win) Difficulties with the new TR-3 hardware, including [regression testing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regression_testing "Regression testing"), have caused delays to Block 4 as well as a halt in aircraft deliveries from July 2023 to July 2024.[&#8202;](#ref-back-95-0)[\[85\]](#refnote-95)[&#8202;](#ref-back-96-0)[\[86\]](#refnote-96)

In 2023, the GAO raised concerns in two reports that the estimated cost of the Block 4 upgrade has increased from $10.6billion to $16.5billion and completion of the upgrade is delayed from 2026 to 2029 at the earliest.[&#8202;](#ref-back-97-0)[\[87\]](#refnote-97) In September 2025, it was announced that the Block 4 upgrade would be truncated and delayed due to technological uncertainties and engine upgrade delays, among other reasons. Critical changes that do not require an upgraded engine will remain in Block 4 and be ready by 2031 at the earliest. Upgrades that were originally planned for Block 4, but require the upgraded engine, or lack technological maturity, will be deferred to currently undefined efforts expected in the mid-2030s. Block 4 was originally intended to be completed in 2026.[&#8202;](#ref-back-98-0)[\[88\]](#refnote-98)[&#8202;](#ref-back-99-0)[\[89\]](#refnote-99)

Defense contractors have offered upgrades to the F-35 outside of official program contracts. In 2013, Northrop Grumman disclosed its development of a directional [infrared countermeasures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infrared_countermeasures "Infrared countermeasures") suite, named Threat Nullification Defensive Resource (ThNDR). The countermeasure system would share the same space as the Distributed Aperture System (DAS) sensors and act as a laser missile jammer to protect against infrared-homing missiles.[&#8202;](#ref-back-100-0)[\[90\]](#refnote-100)

The F-35 is consistently one of the most expensive weapons systems in the [US military budget](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US_military_budget "US military budget"). It was the second-most expensive system funded in the 2025 budget, at $13.3billion, behind the [_Virginia_-class submarine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virginia-class_submarine "Virginia-class submarine") at $13.9billion. It was also the most expensive system in the 2026 budget request, at $13.1billion.[&#8202;](#ref-back-101-0)[\[91\]](#refnote-101)

Israel operates a unique subvariant of the F-35A, designated the F-35I, that is designed to better interface with and incorporate Israeli equipment and weapons. The Israeli Air Force also has their own F-35I test aircraft that provides more access to the core avionics to include their own equipment.[&#8202;](#ref-back-102-0)[\[92\]](#refnote-102)

In June 2026, the US GAO released the report "F-35 Sustainment: Actions Needed to Ensure Updated Strategy Improves Persistent Readiness Challenges". The report found that F-35 fleet readiness had continued to decline despite years of investment. Fleetwide Full Mission Capable rates fell from 38 percent in fiscal year 2021 to approximately 25 percent in fiscal year 2025, while Mission Capable rates declined from 67 percent to 44 percent over the same period. The GAO identified spare parts shortages, insufficient maintenance and depot capacity, engine sustainment issues, and supply chain problems as the main contributing factors. It noted that the Department of Defense launched a $13.7billion sustainment strategy in 2025 intended to improve readiness through 2030, but warned that the plan faces significant implementation risks and its long-term effectiveness remains uncertain.[&#8202;](#ref-back-103-0)[\[93\]](#refnote-103)[&#8202;](#ref-back-104-0)[\[94\]](#refnote-104)[&#8202;](#ref-back-105-0)[\[95\]](#refnote-105)

### Procurement and international participation

Main article: [Lockheed Martin F-35 Lightning II procurement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lockheed_Martin_F-35_Lightning_II_procurement "Lockheed Martin F-35 Lightning II procurement")

The United States is the primary customer and financial backer, with planned procurement of 1,763 F-35As for the USAF, 353 F-35Bs and 67 F-35Cs for the USMC, and 273 F-35Cs for the USN.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FY2020_SAR-1)[\[19\]](#refnote-FY2020_SAR) Additionally, the United Kingdom, Italy, the Netherlands, Turkey, Australia, Norway, Denmark and Canada have agreed to contribute US$4.375billion towards development costs, with the United Kingdom contributing about 10% of the planned development costs as the sole Tier 1 partner.[&#8202;](#ref-back-T1partner-1)[\[26\]](#refnote-T1partner) Britain supplies ejector seats, rear fuselage, active interceptor systems, targeting lasers and weapon release cables, mainly through British Aerospace, amounting to 15% of the value of the F-35, and is the largest supplier of spare parts for the jet after the US.[&#8202;](#ref-back-wintour-0)[\[96\]](#refnote-wintour) The initial plan was that the US and eight major partner countries would acquire over 3,100 F-35s through 2035.[&#8202;](#ref-back-107-0)[\[97\]](#refnote-107) The three tiers of international participation generally reflect financial stake in the program, the amount of technology transfer and subcontracts open for bid by national companies, and the order in which countries can obtain production aircraft.[&#8202;](#ref-back-108-0)[\[98\]](#refnote-108) Alongside program partner countries, Israel and Singapore have joined as Security Cooperative Participants (SCP).[&#8202;](#ref-back-109-0)[\[99\]](#refnote-109)[&#8202;](#ref-back-110-0)[\[100\]](#refnote-110)[&#8202;](#ref-back-111-0)[\[101\]](#refnote-111) Sales to SCP and non-partner states, including Belgium, Japan, and South Korea, are made through the Pentagon's [Foreign Military Sales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_Military_Sales "Foreign Military Sales") program.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:4-1)[\[8\]](#refnote-:4)[&#8202;](#ref-back-112-0)[\[102\]](#refnote-112) Turkey was removed from the F-35 program in July 2019 over security concerns following its purchase of a Russian [S-400](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S-400_missile_system "S-400 missile system") surface-to-air missile system.[&#8202;](#ref-back-113-0)[\[103\]](#refnote-113)[&#8202;](#ref-back-114-0)[\[104\]](#refnote-114)[&#8202;](#ref-back-116-0)[\[N 11\]](#refnote-116) The unit flyaway costs of the 296 aircraft in lots 18-19, for which deliveries start in 2026, are $92M for the F-35A, $121.4M for the F-35B, and $110.8M for the F-35C (2026 dollars).[&#8202;](#ref-back-:18-1)[\[15\]](#refnote-:18) Flyaway costs include all the basic marginal costs to produce one aircraft, including the government-furnished jet engines and radars (Government Furnished Equipment, or GFE, is purchased directly by the government and provided to Lockheed Martin for final assembly). The cost of the F135 engine is $20.4million for lot 18.[&#8202;](#ref-back-117-0)[\[106\]](#refnote-117) Lockheed Martin delivered a record 191 F-35 Lightning II fighter jets in 2025, bringing the global fleet to approximately 1,300 aircraft.\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\]

## Design

### Overview

The F-35 is a family of single-engine, [supersonic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supersonic_aircraft "Supersonic aircraft"), [stealth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stealth_aircraft "Stealth aircraft"), [multirole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multirole_combat_aircraft "Multirole combat aircraft") strike fighters.[&#8202;](#ref-back-LHMC-0)[\[107\]](#refnote-LHMC) The second [fifth-generation fighter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fifth-generation_fighter "Fifth-generation fighter") to enter US service and the first operational supersonic STOVL stealth fighter, the F-35 emphasizes low observables,[&#8202;](#ref-back-119-0)[\[108\]](#refnote-119) advanced avionics, and sensor fusion that enable a high level of situational awareness[&#8202;](#ref-back-120-0)[\[109\]](#refnote-120) and long range lethality;[&#8202;](#ref-back-121-0)[\[110\]](#refnote-121) the USAF considers the aircraft its primary strike fighter for conducting [suppression of enemy air defense](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suppression_of_enemy_air_defense "Suppression of enemy air defense") (SEAD) and [air interdiction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_interdiction "Air interdiction") missions, owing to the advanced sensors and mission systems.[&#8202;](#ref-back-122-0)[\[111\]](#refnote-122)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d5/F-35_Lightning_II_variants_in_flight_near_Eglin_AFB_in_2014.jpg/500px-F-35_Lightning_II_variants_in_flight_near_Eglin_AFB_in_2014.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:F-35_Lightning_II_variants_in_flight_near_Eglin_AFB_in_2014.jpg)_(From the top)_ F-35A of the [33rd FW](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/33rd_Fighter_Wing "33rd Fighter Wing"), F-35B of [VMFAT-501](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VMFAT-501 "VMFAT-501") and F-35C of [VFA-101](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VFA-101 "VFA-101") near [Eglin AFB](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eglin_AFB "Eglin AFB"), 2014[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6d/F-35_Heritage_Flight_Team_performs_in_Bell_Fort_Worth_Alliance_AirShow.jpg/500px-F-35_Heritage_Flight_Team_performs_in_Bell_Fort_Worth_Alliance_AirShow.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:F-35_Heritage_Flight_Team_performs_in_Bell_Fort_Worth_Alliance_AirShow.jpg) A vortex forms around the body of the aircraft

The F-35 has a wing-tail configuration with two vertical stabilizers canted for stealth. Flight control surfaces include [leading-edge flaps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flap_(aeronautics)#Leading_edge_flap "Flap (aeronautics)"), [flaperons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flaperon "Flaperon"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-123-0)[\[N 12\]](#refnote-123) [rudders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rudder "Rudder"), and all-moving horizontal tails ( [stabilators](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stabilator "Stabilator")); [leading edge root extensions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leading_edge_root_extension "Leading edge root extension") or chines[&#8202;](#ref-back-124-0)[\[112\]](#refnote-124) also run forward to the inlets. The relatively short 35-foot wingspan of the F-35A and F-35B is set by the requirement to fit inside USN amphibious assault ship parking areas and elevators; the F-35C's larger wing is more fuel efficient.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ryberg1-0)[\[113\]](#refnote-ryberg1)[&#8202;](#ref-back-126-0)[\[114\]](#refnote-126) The fixed diverterless supersonic inlets (DSI) use a bumped compression surface and forward-swept cowl to shed the boundary layer of the forebody away from the inlets, which form a Y-duct for the engine.[&#8202;](#ref-back-dsi-0)[\[115\]](#refnote-dsi) Structurally, the F-35 drew upon lessons from the F-22; composites comprise 35% of airframe weight, with the majority being [bismaleimide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maleimide "Maleimide") and [composite epoxy materials](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Composite_epoxy_material "Composite epoxy material") as well as some [carbon nanotube](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbon_nanotube "Carbon nanotube")-reinforced [epoxy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epoxy "Epoxy") in later production lots.[&#8202;](#ref-back-128-0)[\[116\]](#refnote-128)[&#8202;](#ref-back-129-0)[\[117\]](#refnote-129)[&#8202;](#ref-back-130-0)[\[118\]](#refnote-130) The F-35 is considerably heavier than the lightweight fighters it replaces, with the lightest variant having an empty weight of 29,300lb (13,300kg); much of the weight can be attributed to the internal weapons bays and the extensive avionics carried.[&#8202;](#ref-back-F-35_skills_analyzed-0)[\[119\]](#refnote-F-35_skills_analyzed) Each F-35 contains about 50 pounds (23kg) of [samarium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samarium "Samarium") magnets.[&#8202;](#ref-back-nyt-bradsher-china-0)[\[120\]](#refnote-nyt-bradsher-china)

While lacking the [kinematic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kinematics "Kinematics") performance of the larger twin-engine F-22, the F-35 is competitive with [fourth-generation fighters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fourth-generation_fighter "Fourth-generation fighter") such as the F-16 and F/A-18, especially when they carry weapons because the F-35's internal weapons bay eliminates drag from external stores.[&#8202;](#ref-back-133-0)[\[121\]](#refnote-133) All variants have a top speed of Mach1.6 (1,220mph; 1,960km/h), attainable with full internal payload. The Pratt & Whitney F135 engine gives good subsonic acceleration and energy, with supersonic dash in afterburner. The F-35, while not a "supercruising" aircraft, can fly at Mach1.2 (913mph; 1,470km/h) for a dash of 150 miles (240km) without afterburner. This ability can be useful in battlefield situations.[&#8202;](#ref-back-134-0)[\[122\]](#refnote-134) The large stabilators, leading edge extensions and flaps, and canted rudders provide excellent high alpha ( [angle-of-attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angle-of-attack "Angle-of-attack")) characteristics, with a trimmed alpha of 50°. [Relaxed stability](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Relaxed_stability "Relaxed stability") and triplex-redundant [fly-by-wire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fly-by-wire "Fly-by-wire") controls provide excellent handling qualities and [departure resistance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Departure_resistance "Departure resistance").[&#8202;](#ref-back-135-0)[\[123\]](#refnote-135)[&#8202;](#ref-back-136-0)[\[124\]](#refnote-136) Having over double the F-16's internal fuel, the F-35 has a considerably greater [combat radius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radius_of_action "Radius of action"), while stealth also enables a more efficient mission flight profile.[&#8202;](#ref-back-LM_F-35_Status_Fast_Facts-0)[\[125\]](#refnote-LM_F-35_Status_Fast_Facts)

The F-35A serves as a central [nuclear weapons delivery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_weapons_delivery "Nuclear weapons delivery") vehicle for US [nuclear sharing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_sharing "Nuclear sharing") in NATO countries, with associated [USAF munitions squadrons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_United_States_Air_Force_munitions_squadrons "List of United States Air Force munitions squadrons") armed with [B61 Mod 12 nuclear bombs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/B61_nuclear_bomb "B61 nuclear bomb"). As of 2026, these F-35A missions are active in the Netherlands at [Volkel Air Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volkel_Air_Base "Volkel Air Base"), and potentially also a USAF mission in the United Kingdom at [RAF Lakenheath](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RAF_Lakenheath "RAF Lakenheath"). Belgium, Italy, and Germany, which also host US nuclear weapons, plan to replace their nuclear-capable aircraft with F-35As.[&#8202;](#ref-back-fasUS2026-0)[\[18\]](#refnote-fasUS2026) As of 2023, [Kleine Brogel Air Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kleine_Brogel_Air_Base "Kleine Brogel Air Base") in Belgium operates [F-16s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Dynamics_F-16_Fighting_Falcon "General Dynamics F-16 Fighting Falcon"), and [Ghedi Air Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghedi_Air_Base "Ghedi Air Base") in Italy and [Büchel Air Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/B%C3%BCchel_Air_Base "Büchel Air Base") in Germany operate [Panavia Tornado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panavia_Tornado "Panavia Tornado") PA-200s, while [Aviano Air Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aviano_Air_Base "Aviano Air Base") in Italy hosts US F-16s. The UK's Royal Air Force also plans to operate its own nuclear sharing mission, [procuring F-35As](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_weapons_of_the_United_Kingdom#F-35A_procurement "Nuclear weapons of the United Kingdom") to be based at [RAF Marham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RAF_Marham "RAF Marham").[&#8202;](#ref-back-fasShare2023-0)[\[126\]](#refnote-fasShare2023)

### Sensors and avionics

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/57/F-35_EOTS_12-5054.jpg/500px-F-35_EOTS_12-5054.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:F-35_EOTS_12-5054.jpg) The AN/AAQ-40 Electro-Optical Target System (EOTS) under the nose of an F-35A

The F-35's mission systems are among the most complex aspects of the aircraft. The avionics and [sensor fusion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sensor_fusion "Sensor fusion") are designed to improve the pilot's [situational awareness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Situational_awareness "Situational awareness") and [command-and-control](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Command_and_control "Command and control") capabilities and facilitate [network-centric warfare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Network-centric_warfare "Network-centric warfare").[&#8202;](#ref-back-LHMC-1)[\[107\]](#refnote-LHMC)[&#8202;](#ref-back-139-0)[\[127\]](#refnote-139) Key sensors include the [Northrop Grumman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northrop_Grumman_Electronic_Systems "Northrop Grumman Electronic Systems") [AN/APG-81](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/APG-81 "APG-81") [active electronically scanned array](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Active_electronically_scanned_array "Active electronically scanned array") (AESA) radar; [BAE Systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BAE_Systems_Electronic_Systems "BAE Systems Electronic Systems") [AN/ASQ-239](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AN/ASQ-239 "AN/ASQ-239") Barracuda [electronic warfare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electronic_warfare "Electronic warfare") system; Northrop Grumman/ [Raytheon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raytheon "Raytheon") [AN/AAQ-37 Electro-optical Distributed Aperture System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AN/AAQ-37_Electro-optical_Distributed_Aperture_System "AN/AAQ-37 Electro-optical Distributed Aperture System") (DAS); Lockheed Martin AN/AAQ-40 Electro-Optical Targeting System (EOTS); and Northrop Grumman AN/ASQ-242 Communications, Navigation, and Identification (CNI) suite. The F-35 was designed for its sensors to work together to provide a cohesive image of the local [battlespace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battlespace "Battlespace"); for example, the APG-81 radar also acts as part of the electronic warfare system.[&#8202;](#ref-back-aviationtoday.com-0)[\[128\]](#refnote-aviationtoday.com)

Much of the F-35's software was developed in [C](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C_(programming_language) "C (programming language)") and [C++](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C++ "C++") [programming languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Programming_language "Programming language"), while [Ada83](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ada_(programming_language) "Ada (programming language)") code from the F-22 was also used; the Block 3F software has 8.6million [lines of code](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Source_lines_of_code "Source lines of code").[&#8202;](#ref-back-141-0)[\[129\]](#refnote-141)[&#8202;](#ref-back-142-0)[\[130\]](#refnote-142) The [Green Hills Software](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_Hills_Software "Green Hills Software") [Integrity DO-178B](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/INTEGRITY-178B "INTEGRITY-178B") [real-time operating system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Real-time_operating_system "Real-time operating system") (RTOS) runs on integrated core processors (ICPs); data networking includes the [IEEE 1394b](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FireWire_800 "FireWire 800") and [Fibre Channel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fibre_Channel "Fibre Channel") buses.[&#8202;](#ref-back-143-0)[\[131\]](#refnote-143)[&#8202;](#ref-back-M&AE_V21_I2_McHale-0)[\[132\]](#refnote-M&AE_V21_I2_McHale) The avionics use [commercial off-the-shelf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commercial_off-the-shelf "Commercial off-the-shelf") (COTS) components when practical to make upgrades cheaper and more flexible; for example, to enable fleet software upgrades for the [software-defined radio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Software-defined_radio "Software-defined radio") (SDR) systems.[&#8202;](#ref-back-avweek_20070205-0)[\[133\]](#refnote-avweek_20070205)[&#8202;](#ref-back-avionics_20070601-0)[\[134\]](#refnote-avionics_20070601)[&#8202;](#ref-back-147-0)[\[135\]](#refnote-147) The mission systems software, particularly for sensor fusion, was one of the program's most difficult parts and responsible for substantial program delays.[&#8202;](#ref-back-149-0)[\[N 13\]](#refnote-149)[&#8202;](#ref-back-150-0)[\[137\]](#refnote-150)[&#8202;](#ref-back-sw_delay-0)[\[138\]](#refnote-sw_delay)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ec/AN-APG-81_Antenna%2C_2005_-_National_Electronics_Museum_-_DSC00393.JPG/500px-AN-APG-81_Antenna%2C_2005_-_National_Electronics_Museum_-_DSC00393.JPG?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:AN-APG-81_Antenna,_2005_-_National_Electronics_Museum_-_DSC00393.JPG) [AN/APG-81](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/APG-81 "APG-81") [AESA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Active_electronically_scanned_array "Active electronically scanned array") radar antenna

The APG-81 radar uses electronic scanning for rapid beam agility and incorporates passive and active air-to-air modes, strike modes, and synthetic aperture radar (SAR) capability, with multiple target [track-while-scan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Track-while-scan "Track-while-scan") at ranges in excess of 80nmi (150km). The antenna is tilted backward for stealth.[&#8202;](#ref-back-152-0)[\[139\]](#refnote-152) Complementing the radar is the AAQ-37 DAS: six [infrared sensors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thermographic_camera "Thermographic camera") that can track targets and [warn of approaching missiles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Missile_approach_warning_system "Missile approach warning system") all around the aircraft; it acts as a situational-awareness [infrared search and track](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infrared_search_and_track "Infrared search and track") (SAIRST) and feeds spherical infrared and night-vision imagery to the helmet visor.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Distributed_Aperture_System-0)[\[140\]](#refnote-Distributed_Aperture_System) The ASQ-239 Barracuda electronic warfare system has ten [radio frequency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radio_frequency "Radio frequency") antennas embedded into the edges of the wing and tail for all-aspect [radar warning receiver](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radar_warning_receiver "Radar warning receiver") (RWR). It also provides sensor fusion of radio frequency and infrared tracking functions, geolocation threat targeting, and [multispectral image](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multispectral_image "Multispectral image") countermeasures for self-defense against missiles. The electronic warfare system can detect and jam hostile radars.[&#8202;](#ref-back-mission_sys_design-0)[\[141\]](#refnote-mission_sys_design) The AAQ-40 EOTS is mounted behind a faceted low-observable window under the nose and performs laser targeting, forward-looking infrared (FLIR), and long-range IRST functions.[&#8202;](#ref-back-155-0)[\[142\]](#refnote-155) The ASQ-242 CNI suite uses a half dozen physical links, including the directional [Multifunction Advanced Data Link](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multifunction_Advanced_Data_Link "Multifunction Advanced Data Link") (MADL), for covert CNI functions.[&#8202;](#ref-back-156-0)[\[143\]](#refnote-156)[&#8202;](#ref-back-157-0)[\[144\]](#refnote-157) Information from radio frequency receivers and infrared sensors is combined to form a single tactical picture for the pilot. The all-aspect target direction and identification can be shared via MADL to other platforms [without compromising low observability](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Low-probability-of-intercept_radar "Low-probability-of-intercept radar"), while [Link 16](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Link_16 "Link 16") enables communication with older systems.[&#8202;](#ref-back-158-0)[\[145\]](#refnote-158)

The F-35 was designed to accept upgrades to its processors, sensors, and software. Technology Refresh 3, which includes a new core processor and a new cockpit display, is planned for Lot 15 aircraft.[&#8202;](#ref-back-159-0)[\[146\]](#refnote-159) Lockheed Martin has offered the Advanced EOTS for the Block 4 configuration; the improved sensor fits into the same area as the baseline EOTS with minimal changes.[&#8202;](#ref-back-160-0)[\[147\]](#refnote-160) In June 2018, Lockheed Martin picked Raytheon for improved DAS.[&#8202;](#ref-back-161-0)[\[148\]](#refnote-161) The USAF has studied the potential for the F-35 to orchestrate attacks by [unmanned combat aerial vehicles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unmanned_combat_aerial_vehicle "Unmanned combat aerial vehicle") (UCAVs) via its sensors and communications equipment.[&#8202;](#ref-back-162-0)[\[149\]](#refnote-162)

A new radar called the [AN/APG-85](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AN/APG-85 "AN/APG-85") is planned for Block 4 F-35s.[&#8202;](#ref-back-auto5-0)[\[150\]](#refnote-auto5) According to the JPO, the new radar will be compatible with all three major F-35 variants. However, it is unclear if older aircraft will be retrofitted with the new radar.[&#8202;](#ref-back-auto5-1)[\[150\]](#refnote-auto5)

### Stealth and signatures

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/14/33rd_FTS%2C_F-35_training_131212-F-TJ158-008.jpg/500px-33rd_FTS%2C_F-35_training_131212-F-TJ158-008.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:33rd_FTS,_F-35_training_131212-F-TJ158-008.jpg) The sawtooth design on the landing gear door and access panels

Stealth is a key aspect of the F-35's design, and [radar cross-section](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radar_cross-section "Radar cross-section") (RCS) is minimized through careful shaping of the airframe and the use of [radar-absorbent materials](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radar-absorbent_material "Radar-absorbent material") (RAM); visible measures to reduce RCS include alignment of edges and continuous curvature of surfaces, serration of skin panels, and the masking of the engine face and turbine. Additionally, the F-35's diverterless supersonic inlet (DSI) uses a compression bump and forward-swept cowl rather than a [splitter gap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Splitter_plate_(aeronautics) "Splitter plate (aeronautics)") or bleed system to divert the [boundary layer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boundary_layer "Boundary layer") away from the inlet duct, eliminating the diverter cavity and further reducing radar signature.[&#8202;](#ref-back-dsi-1)[\[115\]](#refnote-dsi)[&#8202;](#ref-back-164-0)[\[151\]](#refnote-164) The RCS of the F-35 has been characterized as lower than a metal golf ball at certain frequencies and angles; in some conditions, the F-35 compares favorably to the F-22 in stealth.[&#8202;](#ref-back-165-0)[\[152\]](#refnote-165)[&#8202;](#ref-back-166-0)[\[153\]](#refnote-166)[&#8202;](#ref-back-167-0)[\[154\]](#refnote-167) For maintainability, the F-35's stealth design took lessons from earlier stealth aircraft such as the F-22; the F-35's radar-absorbent fibermat skin is more durable and requires less maintenance than older topcoats.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Countermeasures-0)[\[155\]](#refnote-Countermeasures) The aircraft also has reduced [infrared](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infrared_signature "Infrared signature") and [visual signatures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aircraft_camouflage "Aircraft camouflage"), as well as strict controls of radio frequency emitters to prevent their detection.[&#8202;](#ref-back-LockMart_Norway-0)[\[156\]](#refnote-LockMart_Norway)[&#8202;](#ref-back-170-0)[\[157\]](#refnote-170)[&#8202;](#ref-back-171-0)[\[158\]](#refnote-171) The F-35's stealth design is primarily focused on high-frequency [X-band](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/X-band_radar "X-band radar") wavelengths;[&#8202;](#ref-back-172-0)[\[159\]](#refnote-172) [low-frequency radars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Low-frequency_radar "Low-frequency radar") can spot stealthy aircraft due to [Rayleigh scattering](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rayleigh_scattering "Rayleigh scattering"), but such radars are also conspicuous, susceptible to clutter, and lack precision.[&#8202;](#ref-back-173-0)[\[160\]](#refnote-173)[&#8202;](#ref-back-174-0)[\[161\]](#refnote-174)[&#8202;](#ref-back-175-0)[\[162\]](#refnote-175) To disguise its RCS, the aircraft can mount four [Luneburg lens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luneburg_lens "Luneburg lens") reflectors.[&#8202;](#ref-back-176-0)[\[163\]](#refnote-176) Overseas deployments of the F-35, including in Estonia, Bulgaria, and South Korea, use these radar reflectors to prevent Russian and Chinese intelligence gathering on the true RCS signature. There were concerns that [Russian involvement in the Syrian civil war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_involvement_in_the_Syrian_civil_war "Russian involvement in the Syrian civil war") allowed the country to gather true RCS data on Israeli F-35Is flying without reflectors over Lebanon, as well as US F-22s.[&#8202;](#ref-back-auto6-0)[\[164\]](#refnote-auto6)

Noise from the F-35 caused concerns in residential areas near potential bases for the aircraft, and residents near two such bases— [Luke Air Force Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luke_Air_Force_Base "Luke Air Force Base"), Arizona, and [Eglin Air Force Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eglin_Air_Force_Base "Eglin Air Force Base") (AFB), Florida—requested environmental impact studies in 2008 and 2009, respectively.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Alaimo-0)[\[165\]](#refnote-Alaimo) Although the noise levels, in decibels, were comparable to those of prior fighters such as the F-16, the F-35's [sound power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sound_power "Sound power") is stronger—particularly at lower frequencies.[&#8202;](#ref-back-179-0)[\[166\]](#refnote-179) Subsequent surveys and studies have indicated that the noise of the F-35 was not perceptibly different from the F-16 and F/A-18E/F, though the greater low-frequency noise was noticeable for some observers.[&#8202;](#ref-back-180-0)[\[167\]](#refnote-180)[&#8202;](#ref-back-181-0)[\[168\]](#refnote-181)[&#8202;](#ref-back-182-0)[\[169\]](#refnote-182)

### Cockpit

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/51/F-35_training_system%2C_logistic_system_ready_for_operations_150630-M-EG514-000.jpg/500px-F-35_training_system%2C_logistic_system_ready_for_operations_150630-M-EG514-000.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:F-35_training_system,_logistic_system_ready_for_operations_150630-M-EG514-000.jpg) F-35 cockpit simulator

The [glass cockpit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glass_cockpit "Glass cockpit") was designed to give the pilot good situational awareness. The main display is a 20-by-8-inch (50 by 20cm) panoramic [touchscreen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Touchscreen "Touchscreen"), which shows flight instruments, stores management, CNI information, and integrated caution and warnings; the pilot can customize the arrangement of the information. Below the main display is a smaller stand-by display.[&#8202;](#ref-back-183-0)[\[170\]](#refnote-183) The cockpit has a [speech-recognition system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Direct_Voice_Input "Direct Voice Input") developed by [Adacel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adacel "Adacel").[&#8202;](#ref-back-voice-0)[\[171\]](#refnote-voice) The F-35 does not have a [head-up display](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Head-up_display "Head-up display"); instead, flight and combat information is displayed on the [visor of the pilot's helmet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helmet-mounted_display "Helmet-mounted display") in a helmet-mounted display system (HMDS).[&#8202;](#ref-back-hdms-0)[\[172\]](#refnote-hdms) The one-piece tinted canopy is hinged at the front and has an internal frame for structural strength. The [Martin-Baker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martin-Baker "Martin-Baker") US16E [ejection seat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ejection_seat "Ejection seat") is launched by a twin-catapult system housed on side rails.[&#8202;](#ref-back-186-0)[\[173\]](#refnote-186) There is a right-hand [side stick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Side-stick "Side-stick") and throttle [hands-on throttle-and-stick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HOTAS "HOTAS") system. For life support, an [onboard oxygen-generation system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Onboard_oxygen-generation_system?action=edit&redlink=1 "Onboard oxygen-generation system") (OBOGS) is fitted and powered by the Integrated Power Package (IPP), with an auxiliary oxygen bottle and backup oxygen system for emergencies.[&#8202;](#ref-back-187-0)[\[174\]](#refnote-187)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a6/U.S._Air_Force_Capt._Brad_Matherne%2C_a_pilot_with_the_422nd_Test_and_Evaluation_Squadron%2C_taxis_an_F-35A_Lightning_II_aircraft_before_its_first_operational_training_mission_April_4%2C_2013%2C_at_Nellis_Air_Force_130404-F-KX404-037.jpg/500px-thumbnail.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:U.S._Air_Force_Capt._Brad_Matherne,_a_pilot_with_the_422nd_Test_and_Evaluation_Squadron,_taxis_an_F-35A_Lightning_II_aircraft_before_its_first_operational_training_mission_April_4,_2013,_at_Nellis_Air_Force_130404-F-KX404-037.jpg) The F-35's helmet-mounted display system

The Vision Systems International[&#8202;](#ref-back-188-0)[\[N 14\]](#refnote-188) helmet display is a key piece of the F-35's human-machine interface. Instead of the head-up display mounted atop the dashboard of earlier fighters, the HMDS puts flight and combat information on the helmet visor, allowing the pilot to see it no matter which way they are facing.[&#8202;](#ref-back-189-0)[\[175\]](#refnote-189) Infrared and night vision imagery from the Distributed Aperture System can be displayed directly on the HMDS and enables the pilot to "see through" the aircraft. The HMDS allows an F-35 pilot to fire missiles at targets even when the nose of the aircraft is pointing elsewhere by cuing missile seekers at high angles off-boresight.[&#8202;](#ref-back-F-35_prog_brief_Sept2006-0)[\[176\]](#refnote-F-35_prog_brief_Sept2006)[&#8202;](#ref-back-191-0)[\[177\]](#refnote-191) Each helmet costs $400,000.[&#8202;](#ref-back-192-0)[\[178\]](#refnote-192) The HMDS weighs more than traditional helmets, and there is concern that it can endanger lightweight pilots during ejection.[&#8202;](#ref-back-193-0)[\[179\]](#refnote-193)

Due to the HMDS's vibration, jitter, night-vision and sensor display problems during development, Lockheed Martin and Elbit issued a draft specification in 2011 for an alternative HMDS based on the AN/AVS-9 [night vision](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Night_vision "Night vision") goggles as backup, with BAE Systems chosen later that year.[&#8202;](#ref-back-194-0)[\[180\]](#refnote-194)[&#8202;](#ref-back-195-0)[\[181\]](#refnote-195) A cockpit redesign would be needed to adopt an alternative HMDS.[&#8202;](#ref-back-196-0)[\[182\]](#refnote-196)[&#8202;](#ref-back-197-0)[\[183\]](#refnote-197) Following progress on the baseline helmet, development on the alternative HMDS was halted in October 2013.[&#8202;](#ref-back-C4WDefault-2)[\[184\]](#refnote-C4WDefault)[&#8202;](#ref-back-199-0)[\[185\]](#refnote-199) In 2016, the Gen 3 helmet with improved night vision camera, new liquid crystal displays, automated alignment and software enhancements was introduced with LRIP lot 7.[&#8202;](#ref-back-C4WDefault-3)[\[184\]](#refnote-C4WDefault)

### Armament

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9d/190116-F-BQ566-003.jpg/500px-190116-F-BQ566-003.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:190116-F-BQ566-003.jpg) F-35A with all weapon bay doors open

To preserve its stealth shaping, the F-35 has two internal [weapons bays](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bomb_bay "Bomb bay"), each with two weapons stations. The two [outboard weapon stations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hardpoint "Hardpoint") each can carry ordnance up to 2,500lb (1,100kg), or 1,500lb (680kg) for the F-35B, while the two inboard stations carry air-to-air missiles. Air-to-surface weapons for the outboard station include the [Joint Direct Attack Munition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint_Direct_Attack_Munition "Joint Direct Attack Munition") (JDAM), [Paveway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paveway "Paveway") series of bombs, [Joint Standoff Weapon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AGM-154_Joint_Standoff_Weapon "AGM-154 Joint Standoff Weapon") (JSOW), and [cluster munitions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cluster_munition "Cluster munition") ( [Wind Corrected Munitions Dispenser](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wind_Corrected_Munitions_Dispenser "Wind Corrected Munitions Dispenser")). The station can also carry multiple smaller munitions, such as the [GBU-39 Small Diameter Bombs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GBU-39_Small_Diameter_Bomb "GBU-39 Small Diameter Bomb") (SDB), [GBU-53/B StormBreaker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GBU-53/B_StormBreaker "GBU-53/B StormBreaker") and [SPEAR 3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SPEAR_3 "SPEAR 3"); up to four SDBs can be carried per station for the F-35A and F-35C, and three for the F-35B.[&#8202;](#ref-back-200-0)[\[186\]](#refnote-200)[&#8202;](#ref-back-201-0)[\[187\]](#refnote-201)[&#8202;](#ref-back-mbda_spear-0)[\[188\]](#refnote-mbda_spear) The F-35A achieved certification to carry the [B61 Mod 12 nuclear bomb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/B61_nuclear_bomb#B61_Mod_12 "B61 nuclear bomb") in October 2023.[&#8202;](#ref-back-B61_certified-0)[\[189\]](#refnote-B61_certified) The inboard station can carry the [AIM-120 AMRAAM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AIM-120_AMRAAM "AIM-120 AMRAAM") and eventually the [AIM-260 JATM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AIM-260_JATM "AIM-260 JATM"). Two compartments behind the weapons bays contain [flares](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flare_(countermeasure) "Flare (countermeasure)"), [chaff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chaff_(countermeasure) "Chaff (countermeasure)"), and towed decoys.[&#8202;](#ref-back-204-0)[\[190\]](#refnote-204)

An F-35A from the 4th Fighter Squadron, 388th Fighter Wing, Hill AFB, Utah, flies a strafing run at the Utah Test & Training Range in August 2018. This was the first operation use of the [GAU-22/A](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GAU-22/A "GAU-22/A") of the F-35A aircraft.

The aircraft can use six external weapons stations for missions that do not require stealth;[&#8202;](#ref-back-205-0)[\[191\]](#refnote-205) a configuration dubbed "beast mode" by Lockheed Martin.[&#8202;](#ref-back-206-0)[\[192\]](#refnote-206) The wingtip pylons each can carry an [AIM-9X](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AIM-9_Sidewinder "AIM-9 Sidewinder") or [AIM-132 ASRAAM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ASRAAM "ASRAAM") and are canted outwards to reduce their radar cross-section.[&#8202;](#ref-back-UK_for_ASRAAM-0)[\[193\]](#refnote-UK_for_ASRAAM)[&#8202;](#ref-back-208-0)[\[194\]](#refnote-208) Additionally, each wing has a 5,000lb (2,300kg) inboard station and a 2,500lb (1,100kg) middle station, or 1,500lb (680kg) for F-35B. The external wing stations can carry large air-to-surface weapons that would not fit inside the weapons bays such as the [AGM-158 Joint Air to Surface Standoff Missile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AGM-158_JASSM "AGM-158 JASSM") (JASSM) or [AGM-158C LRASM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AGM-158C_LRASM "AGM-158C LRASM") cruise missile.
