An air-to-air missile load of eight AIM-120s and two AIM-9s is possible using internal and external weapons stations; a configuration of six 2,000lb (910kg) bombs, two AIM-120s and two AIM-9s can also be arranged.[&#8202;](#ref-back-F-35_prog_brief_Sept2006-1)[\[176\]](#refnote-F-35_prog_brief_Sept2006)[&#8202;](#ref-back-209-0)[\[195\]](#refnote-209)[&#8202;](#ref-back-JSFrange-0)[\[196\]](#refnote-JSFrange) The F-35 is armed with a [GAU-22/A rotary cannon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GAU-22/A "GAU-22/A"), a lighter four-barrel variant of the [25mm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/25_mm_caliber "25 mm caliber") GAU-12/U Equalizer.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Multimission_pod-0)[\[197\]](#refnote-Multimission_pod) On the F-35A, this is mounted internally near the left wing root with 182 rounds carried;[&#8202;](#ref-back-212-0)[\[198\]](#refnote-212)[&#8202;](#ref-back-213-0)[\[199\]](#refnote-213) the gun is more effective against ground targets than the [20 mm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/20_mm_caliber "20 mm caliber") gun carried by other USAF fighters.\[ _[dubious](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Accuracy_dispute#Disputed_statement "Wikipedia:Accuracy dispute")– [discuss](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talk:Lockheed_Martin_F-35_Lightning_II#Dubious "Talk:Lockheed Martin F-35 Lightning II")_\]\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\] In 2020, a USAF report noted "unacceptable" accuracy problems with the GAU-22/A on the F-35A. These were due to "misalignments" in the gun's mount, which was also susceptible to cracking.[&#8202;](#ref-back-gun_cracking-0)[\[200\]](#refnote-gun_cracking) These problems were resolved by 2024.[&#8202;](#ref-back-GAU-22/A_resolved-0)[\[201\]](#refnote-GAU-22/A_resolved) The F-35B and F-35C have no internal gun and instead can use a [Terma A/S](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terma_A/S "Terma A/S") multi-mission pod (MMP) carrying the GAU-22/A and 220 rounds; the pod is mounted on the centerline of the aircraft and shaped to reduce its radar cross-section.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Multimission_pod-1)[\[197\]](#refnote-Multimission_pod)[&#8202;](#ref-back-216-0)[\[202\]](#refnote-216)\[ _[verification needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability "Wikipedia:Verifiability")_\] In lieu of the gun, the pod  can also be used for different equipment and purposes, such as [electronic warfare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electronic_warfare "Electronic warfare"), [aerial reconnaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aerial_reconnaissance "Aerial reconnaissance"), or rear-facing tactical radar.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Multi-Mission_Pod-0)[\[203\]](#refnote-Multi-Mission_Pod)[&#8202;](#ref-back-f-16.net-0)[\[204\]](#refnote-f-16.net) The pod was not susceptible to the accuracy issues that once plagued the gun on the F-35A variant,[&#8202;](#ref-back-gun_cracking-1)[\[200\]](#refnote-gun_cracking) though it was apparently not problem-free.[&#8202;](#ref-back-GAU-22/A_resolved-1)[\[201\]](#refnote-GAU-22/A_resolved)

Lockheed Martin is developing a weapon rack called Sidekick that would enable the internal outboard station to carry two AIM-120s, thus increasing the internal air-to-air payload to six missiles, currently offered for Block 4.[&#8202;](#ref-back-219-0)[\[205\]](#refnote-219)[&#8202;](#ref-back-dec2010-0)[\[206\]](#refnote-dec2010) Block 4 will also have a rearranged hydraulic line and bracket to allow the F-35B to carry four SDBs per internal outboard station; integration of the MBDA [Meteor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meteor_(missile) "Meteor (missile)") is also planned.[&#8202;](#ref-back-clipped-fin-0)[\[207\]](#refnote-clipped-fin)[&#8202;](#ref-back-222-0)[\[208\]](#refnote-222) The USAF and USN are planning to integrate the [AGM-88G AARGM-ER](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AGM-88_HARM#AGM-88G_AARGM-ER "AGM-88 HARM") internally in the F-35A and F-35C.[&#8202;](#ref-back-223-0)[\[209\]](#refnote-223) Norway and Australia are funding an adaptation of the [Naval Strike Missile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naval_Strike_Missile "Naval Strike Missile") (NSM) for the F-35; designated [Joint Strike Missile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint_Strike_Missile "Joint Strike Missile") (JSM), two missiles can be carried internally with an additional four externally.[&#8202;](#ref-back-224-0)[\[210\]](#refnote-224) Both [hypersonic missiles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hypersonic_weapon "Hypersonic weapon") and [directed-energy weapons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Directed-energy_weapons "Directed-energy weapons") such as [solid-state lasers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solid-state_laser "Solid-state laser") are currently being considered as future upgrades; in 2024, Lockheed Martin disclosed its proposed [Mako](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mako_(missile) "Mako (missile)") hypersonic missile, which can be carried internally in the F-35A and C and externally on the B.[&#8202;](#ref-back-225-0)[\[211\]](#refnote-225)[&#8202;](#ref-back-229-0)[\[N 15\]](#refnote-229)[&#8202;](#ref-back-230-0)[\[215\]](#refnote-230) Additionally, Lockheed Martin is studying integrating a [fiber laser](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fiber_laser "Fiber laser") that uses spectral beam combining multiple individual laser modules into a single high-power beam, which can be scaled to various levels.[&#8202;](#ref-back-231-0)[\[216\]](#refnote-231)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/04/F-35A_with_2_B61-12_bombs.jpg/500px-F-35A_with_2_B61-12_bombs.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:F-35A_with_2_B61-12_bombs.jpg) F-35A weapon bays with two [GBU-31(V)3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint_Direct_Attack_Munition#Variants "Joint Direct Attack Munition") bombs and 2 AIM-120 AMRAAM

The USAF plans for the F-35A to take up the [close air support](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Close_air_support "Close air support") (CAS) mission in contested environments; amid criticism that it is not as well suited as a dedicated attack platform, USAF chief of staff [Mark Welsh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_Welsh "Mark Welsh") placed a focus on weapons for CAS sorties, including [guided rockets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydra_70#Precision_guided_Hydra_70 "Hydra 70"), fragmentation rockets that shatter into individual projectiles before impact, and more compact ammunition for higher capacity gun pods.[&#8202;](#ref-back-232-0)[\[217\]](#refnote-232) Fragmentary rocket warheads create greater effects than cannon shells as each rocket creates a "thousand-round burst", delivering more projectiles than a [strafing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strafing "Strafing") run.[&#8202;](#ref-back-233-0)[\[218\]](#refnote-233)

As of 2022, the most common armaments across air forces are the AIM-120 AMRAAM, used by all F-35 operators except the Czech Republic and Finland, and the [GBU-31](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GBU-31 "GBU-31") family of 2,000-pound JDAM bombs, used by all operators except the Czech Republic and the United Kingdom.[&#8202;](#ref-back-o959-0)[\[219\]](#refnote-o959)

### Engine

The aircraft is powered by a single [Pratt & Whitney F135](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pratt_&_Whitney_F135 "Pratt & Whitney F135") low-bypass augmented turbofan with a rated thrust of 28,000lbf (125kN) at military power and 43,000lbf (191kN) with afterburner. Derived from the [Pratt & Whitney F119](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pratt_&_Whitney_F119 "Pratt & Whitney F119") used by the F-22, the F135 has a larger fan and higher bypass ratio to increase subsonic thrust and [fuel efficiency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fuel_efficiency "Fuel efficiency"), and unlike the F119, is not optimized for [supercruise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supercruise "Supercruise").[&#8202;](#ref-back-JSF-FAQ-0)[\[220\]](#refnote-JSF-FAQ) The engine contributes to the F-35's stealth by having a low-observable augmenter, or [afterburner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afterburner "Afterburner"), that incorporates fuel injectors into thick curved vanes; these vanes are covered by ceramic radar-absorbent materials and mask the turbine. The stealthy augmenter had problems with pressure pulsations, or "screech", at low altitude and high speed early in its development.[&#8202;](#ref-back-236-0)[\[221\]](#refnote-236) The low-observable axisymmetric nozzle consists of 15 partially overlapping flaps that create a sawtooth pattern at the trailing edge, which reduces radar signature and creates shed vortices that reduce the infrared signature of the exhaust plume.[&#8202;](#ref-back-237-0)[\[222\]](#refnote-237) The engine's large size forced the Navy to change its underway replenishment system to bring new engines aboard at sea.[&#8202;](#ref-back-238-0)[\[223\]](#refnote-238) The F-35's Integrated Power Package (IPP) performs power and thermal management and integrates environmental control, auxiliary power unit, engine starting, and other functions into a single system.[&#8202;](#ref-back-av_tech-0)[\[224\]](#refnote-av_tech)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/55/F-35B_Joint_Strike_Fighter_%28thrust_vectoring_nozzle_and_lift_fan%29.PNG/500px-F-35B_Joint_Strike_Fighter_%28thrust_vectoring_nozzle_and_lift_fan%29.PNG?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:F-35B_Joint_Strike_Fighter_(thrust_vectoring_nozzle_and_lift_fan).PNG) Illustration of the [STOVL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/STOVL "STOVL") swivel nozzle, lift fan, and roll-control posts

The F135-PW-600 variant for the F-35B incorporates the Shaft-Driven Lift Fan (SDLF) to allow [STOVL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/STOVL "STOVL") operations. Designed by Lockheed Martin and developed by [Rolls-Royce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rolls-Royce_Holdings "Rolls-Royce Holdings"), the SDLF, also known as the [Rolls-Royce LiftSystem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rolls-Royce_LiftSystem "Rolls-Royce LiftSystem"), consists of the lift fan, drive shaft, two roll posts, and a "three-bearing swivel module" (3BSM). The [nozzle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nozzle "Nozzle") features three [bearings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bearing_(mechanical) "Bearing (mechanical)") resembling a short [cylinder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cylinder "Cylinder") with [nonparallel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parallel_(geometry) "Parallel (geometry)") bases. As the [toothed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gear "Gear") edges are rotated by motors, the nozzle swivels from being [linear](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Linearity "Linearity") with the engine to being [perpendicular](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perpendicular "Perpendicular"). The [thrust vectoring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thrust_vectoring "Thrust vectoring") 3BSM nozzle allows the main engine exhaust to be deflected downward at the tail of the aircraft and is moved by a ["fueldraulic" actuator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valve_actuator "Valve actuator") that uses pressurized fuel as the [working fluid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Working_fluid "Working fluid").[&#8202;](#ref-back-240-0)[\[225\]](#refnote-240)[&#8202;](#ref-back-241-0)[\[226\]](#refnote-241)[&#8202;](#ref-back-242-0)[\[227\]](#refnote-242) Unlike the Harrier's [Pegasus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rolls-Royce_Pegasus "Rolls-Royce Pegasus") engine that entirely uses direct engine thrust for lift, the F-35B's system augments the swivel nozzle's thrust with the lift fan; the fan is powered by the low-pressure turbine through a drive shaft when engaged with a clutch and placed near the front of the aircraft to provide a torque countering that of the 3BSM nozzle.[&#8202;](#ref-back-243-0)[\[228\]](#refnote-243)[&#8202;](#ref-back-244-0)[\[229\]](#refnote-244)[&#8202;](#ref-back-245-0)[\[230\]](#refnote-245) Roll control during slow flight is achieved by diverting unheated engine [bypass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bypass_duct "Bypass duct") air through wing-mounted thrust nozzles called roll posts.[&#8202;](#ref-back-lift1-0)[\[231\]](#refnote-lift1)[&#8202;](#ref-back-247-0)[\[232\]](#refnote-247)

An alternative engine, the [General Electric/Allison/Rolls-Royce F136](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Electric/Rolls-Royce_F136 "General Electric/Rolls-Royce F136"), was being developed in the 1990s and 2000s; originally, F-35 engines from Lot 6 onward were competitively tendered. Using technology from the [General Electric YF120](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Electric_YF120 "General Electric YF120"), the F136 was claimed to have a greater temperature margin than the F135 due to the higher mass flow design making full use of the inlet.[&#8202;](#ref-back-thrust_target-1)[\[42\]](#refnote-thrust_target)[&#8202;](#ref-back-248-0)[\[233\]](#refnote-248) The F136 was canceled in December 2011 due to lack of funding.[&#8202;](#ref-back-249-0)[\[234\]](#refnote-249)[&#8202;](#ref-back-250-0)[\[235\]](#refnote-250)

The F-35 is expected to receive propulsion upgrades over its lifecycle to adapt to emerging threats and enable additional capabilities. In 2016, the Adaptive Engine Transition Program (AETP) was launched to develop and test adaptive cycle engines, with one major potential application being the re-engining of the F-35; in 2018, both GE and P&W were awarded contracts to develop 45,000lbf (200kN) thrust class demonstrators, with the designations [XA100](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/XA100 "XA100") and [XA101](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/XA101 "XA101") respectively.[&#8202;](#ref-back-fg20180709-1)[\[82\]](#refnote-fg20180709) In addition to potential re-engining, P&W is also developing improvements to the baseline F135; the Engine Core Upgrade (ECU) is an update to the power module, originally called Growth Option 1.0 and then Engine Enhancement Package, that improves engine thrust and fuel burn by 5% and bleed air cooling capacity by 50% to support Block 4.[&#8202;](#ref-back-251-0)[\[236\]](#refnote-251)[&#8202;](#ref-back-252-0)[\[237\]](#refnote-252)[&#8202;](#ref-back-253-0)[\[238\]](#refnote-253) The F135 ECU was selected over AETP engines in 2023 to provide additional power and cooling for the F-35. Although GE had expected that the more revolutionary XA100 could enter service with the F-35A and C by 2027 and could be adapted for the F-35B, the increased cost and risk caused the USAF to choose the F135 ECU instead.[&#8202;](#ref-back-254-0)[\[239\]](#refnote-254)[&#8202;](#ref-back-f35_pw_ecu_win-1)[\[84\]](#refnote-f35_pw_ecu_win)

### Maintenance and logistics

The F-35 is designed to require less maintenance than earlier stealth aircraft. Some 95% of all field-replaceable parts are "one deep"—that is, nothing else needs to be removed to reach the desired part; for instance, the ejection seat can be replaced without removing the canopy. The F-35 has a fibermat radar-absorbent material (RAM) baked into the skin, which is more durable, easier to work with, and faster to cure than older RAM coatings; similar coatings are being considered for application on older stealth aircraft such as the F-22.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Countermeasures-1)[\[155\]](#refnote-Countermeasures)[&#8202;](#ref-back-raptorroadmap2019-0)[\[240\]](#refnote-raptorroadmap2019)[&#8202;](#ref-back-256-0)[\[241\]](#refnote-256) Skin corrosion on the F-22 led to the F-35 using a less [galvanic corrosion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galvanic_corrosion "Galvanic corrosion")-inducing skin gap filler, fewer gaps in the airframe skin needing filler, and better drainage.[&#8202;](#ref-back-257-0)[\[242\]](#refnote-257) The flight control system uses [electro-hydrostatic actuators](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electro-hydrostatic_actuator "Electro-hydrostatic actuator") rather than traditional hydraulic systems; these controls can be powered by lithium-ion batteries in an emergency.[&#8202;](#ref-back-258-0)[\[243\]](#refnote-258)[&#8202;](#ref-back-259-0)[\[244\]](#refnote-259)[&#8202;](#ref-back-260-0)[\[245\]](#refnote-260) Commonality between variants led to the USMC's first aircraft maintenance Field Training Detachment, which applied USAF lessons to their F-35 operations.[&#8202;](#ref-back-261-0)[\[246\]](#refnote-261)

The F-35 was initially supported by a computerized maintenance management system named Autonomic Logistics Information System (ALIS). In concept, any F-35 can be serviced at any maintenance facility and all parts can be globally tracked and shared as needed.[&#8202;](#ref-back-262-0)[\[247\]](#refnote-262) In September 2026, a shipment of F-35 components sent from Australia to the United States for repair was diverted to Hong Kong, prompting investigations by U.S. and Australian authorities amid concerns that the components could potentially expose sensitive F-35 technology. The Pentagon's F-35 Joint Program Office said it was working with U.S. authorities and industry partners to retrieve the components and investigate the incident.[&#8202;](#ref-back-F35HongKongABC-0)[\[248\]](#refnote-F35HongKongABC)[&#8202;](#ref-back-264-0)[\[249\]](#refnote-264) Australia said it was assisting the investigation, while its defence minister said the missing components were not believed to be sensitive.[&#8202;](#ref-back-F35HongKongABC-1)[\[248\]](#refnote-F35HongKongABC) Due to numerous problems,[&#8202;](#ref-back-55pctCapable-0)[\[250\]](#refnote-55pctCapable) such as unreliable diagnoses, excessive connectivity requirements, and [security vulnerabilities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vulnerability_(computing) "Vulnerability (computing)"), ALIS is being replaced by the cloud-based Operational Data Integrated Network (ODIN).[&#8202;](#ref-back-266-0)[\[251\]](#refnote-266)[&#8202;](#ref-back-267-0)[\[252\]](#refnote-267)[&#8202;](#ref-back-268-0)[\[253\]](#refnote-268) From September 2020, ODIN base kits (OBKs)[&#8202;](#ref-back-paxTester-0)[\[254\]](#refnote-paxTester) were running ALIS software, as well as ODIN software, first at Marine Corps Air Station (MCAS) Yuma, Arizona, then at Naval Air Station Lemoore, California, in support of Strike Fighter Squadron (VFA) 125 on 16 July 2021, and then Nellis Air Force Base, Nevada, in support of the 422nd Test and Evaluation Squadron (TES) on 6 August 2021. In 2022, over a dozen more OBK sites will replace the ALIS's Standard Operating Unit unclassified (SOU-U) servers.[&#8202;](#ref-back-colin-0)[\[255\]](#refnote-colin) OBK performance is double that of ALIS.[&#8202;](#ref-back-dvids-0)[\[256\]](#refnote-dvids)[&#8202;](#ref-back-colin-1)[\[255\]](#refnote-colin)[&#8202;](#ref-back-paxTester-1)[\[254\]](#refnote-paxTester)

## Operational history

### Testing

The first F-35A, AA-1, conducted its engine run in September 2006 and first flew on 15 December 2006.[&#8202;](#ref-back-jsf_engine_run-0)[\[257\]](#refnote-jsf_engine_run) Unlike subsequent aircraft, AA-1 did not have the weight optimization from SWAT, so it mainly tested subsystems common to subsequent aircraft, such as the propulsion, electrical system, and cockpit displays. This aircraft was retired from flight testing in December 2009 and was used for live-fire testing at [NAS China Lake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NAS_China_Lake "NAS China Lake").[&#8202;](#ref-back-273-0)[\[258\]](#refnote-273)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/43/First_F-35_headed_for_USAF_service.jpg/500px-First_F-35_headed_for_USAF_service.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:First_F-35_headed_for_USAF_service.jpg) The first delivered USAF F-35A on its delivery flight to Eglin AFB, July 2011

The first F-35B, BF-1, first flew on 11 June 2008, first hovered on 17 March 2010, and first landed vertically the next day.[&#8202;](#ref-back-274-0)[\[259\]](#refnote-274) The first weight-optimized F-35A, AF-1, flew on 14 November 2009, and the first such F-35C, CF-1, on 6 June 2010. The F-35 Integrated Test Force (ITF) consisted of 18 aircraft at [Edwards Air Force Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edwards_Air_Force_Base "Edwards Air Force Base") and [Naval Air Station Patuxent River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naval_Air_Station_Patuxent_River "Naval Air Station Patuxent River"). At Edwards, five F-35As, three F-35Bs, and one F-35 performed flight sciences testing such as F-35A [envelope](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flight_envelope "Flight envelope") expansion, flight loads, stores separation, as well as mission systems testing. At Patuxent River, five F-35Bs and four F-35Cs handled their variants' envelope expansion and STOVL and CV suitability testing. Additional carrier suitability testing was conducted at [Naval Air Warfare Center Aircraft Division](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naval_Air_Warfare_Center_Aircraft_Division "Naval Air Warfare Center Aircraft Division") at [Lakehurst, New Jersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lakehurst,_New_Jersey "Lakehurst, New Jersey"). Two non-flying aircraft of each variant were used to test static loads and fatigue.[&#8202;](#ref-back-sdd_flt_test-0)[\[260\]](#refnote-sdd_flt_test) The [Lockheed Martin CATBird](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lockheed_Martin_CATBird "Lockheed Martin CATBird"), a modified [Boeing 737-300](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boeing_737_Classic "Boeing 737 Classic") with a duplication of the cockpit, has been used to test avionics and mission systems.[&#8202;](#ref-back-dec2010-1)[\[206\]](#refnote-dec2010) Field testing of the F-35's sensors was conducted during [Exercise Northern Edge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exercise_Northern_Edge "Exercise Northern Edge") 2009 and 2011, serving as risk-reduction steps.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Branch25Jun09-0)[\[261\]](#refnote-Branch25Jun09)[&#8202;](#ref-back-277-0)[\[262\]](#refnote-277)

Flight tests revealed deficiencies that caused delays, costly redesigns, and several fleet-wide groundings. In 2011, the F-35C failed to catch the arresting wire in all eight landing tests;[&#8202;](#ref-back-278-0)[\[263\]](#refnote-278) a redesigned tail hook was delivered two years later.[&#8202;](#ref-back-newhook-0)[\[264\]](#refnote-newhook) By June 2009, many of the initial flight test targets had been accomplished, but the program was behind schedule.[&#8202;](#ref-back-DTI-0)[\[265\]](#refnote-DTI) Software and mission systems were among the biggest sources of delays for the program, with sensor fusion proving especially challenging.[&#8202;](#ref-back-sw_delay-1)[\[138\]](#refnote-sw_delay) In fatigue testing, the F-35B suffered several premature cracks, requiring a redesign of the structure.[&#8202;](#ref-back-281-0)[\[266\]](#refnote-281) A third non-flying F-35B was planned to test the redesigned structure. Prolonged afterburner use was found to damage the horizontal tails of the F-35B and C.[&#8202;](#ref-back-284-0)[\[N 16\]](#refnote-284)[&#8202;](#ref-back-FY2019DOTE-0)[\[269\]](#refnote-FY2019DOTE)[&#8202;](#ref-back-286-0)[\[270\]](#refnote-286) Early flight control laws had problems with "wing drop"[&#8202;](#ref-back-287-0)[\[N 17\]](#refnote-287) and also made the airplane sluggish, with high angles-of-attack tests in 2015 against an F-16 showing a lack of energy.[&#8202;](#ref-back-288-0)[\[271\]](#refnote-288)[&#8202;](#ref-back-289-0)[\[272\]](#refnote-289)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fc/U.S._Marine_Corps_F-35B_Lands_on_JS_Izumo._Image_17_of_35.jpg/500px-U.S._Marine_Corps_F-35B_Lands_on_JS_Izumo._Image_17_of_35.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:U.S._Marine_Corps_F-35B_Lands_on_JS_Izumo._Image_17_of_35.jpg) US Marine Corps F-35B lands on [JS _Izumo_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/JS_Izumo "JS Izumo")

At-sea testing of the F-35B was first conducted aboard [USS _Wasp_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS_Wasp_(LHD-1) "USS Wasp (LHD-1)"). In October 2011, two F-35Bs conducted three weeks of initial sea trials, called Development Test I.[&#8202;](#ref-back-290-0)[\[273\]](#refnote-290) The second F-35B sea trials, Development Test II, began in August 2013; two aircraft completed 19 nighttime vertical landings using DAS imagery.[&#8202;](#ref-back-DTII-0)[\[274\]](#refnote-DTII)[&#8202;](#ref-back-292-0)[\[275\]](#refnote-292) The first operational testing involving six F-35Bs was done on the _Wasp_ in May 2015. The final Development Test III on [USS _America_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS_America_(LHA-6) "USS America (LHA-6)"), with operations in high sea states, was completed in late 2016.[&#8202;](#ref-back-293-0)[\[276\]](#refnote-293) A Royal Navy F-35 conducted the first "rolling" landing on board [HMS _Queen Elizabeth_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HMS_Queen_Elizabeth_(R08) "HMS Queen Elizabeth (R08)") in October 2018.[&#8202;](#ref-back-294-0)[\[277\]](#refnote-294)

USN F-35C makes the aircraft's first arrested landing aboard the carrier [USS _Nimitz_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS_Nimitz "USS Nimitz") off the coast of San Diego

After the redesigned tail hook arrived, the F-35C's carrier-based Development Test I began in November 2014 aboard [USS _Nimitz_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS_Nimitz "USS Nimitz"); it focused on basic day carrier operations and establishing launch and recovery handling procedures.[&#8202;](#ref-back-295-0)[\[278\]](#refnote-295) Development Test II, which focused on night operations, weapons loading, and full power launches, took place in October 2015. The final Development Test III was completed in August 2016, and included tests of asymmetric loads and certifying systems for landing qualifications and interoperability.[&#8202;](#ref-back-296-0)[\[279\]](#refnote-296) Operational test of the F-35C was conducted in 2018, and the first operational squadron achieved safe-for-flight milestone that December, paving the way for its introduction in 2019.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:0-2)[\[4\]](#refnote-:0)[&#8202;](#ref-back-297-0)[\[280\]](#refnote-297)

The F-35's reliability and availability have fallen short of requirements, especially in the early years of testing. The ALIS maintenance and logistics system was plagued by excessive connectivity requirements and faulty diagnoses. In late 2017, the GAO reported the time needed to repair an F-35 part averaged 172 days, which was "twice the program's objective", and that shortage of spare parts was degrading readiness.[&#8202;](#ref-back-shortage-0)[\[281\]](#refnote-shortage) In 2019, while individual F-35 units have achieved mission-capable rates of over the target of 80% for short periods during deployed operations, fleet-wide rates remained below target. The fleet availability goal of 65% was also not met, although the trend shows improvement. Internal gun accuracy of the F-35A was unacceptable until misalignment issues were addressed by 2024.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FY2019DOTE-1)[\[269\]](#refnote-FY2019DOTE)[&#8202;](#ref-back-299-0)[\[282\]](#refnote-299) As of 2020, the number of the program's most serious issues has been decreased by half.[&#8202;](#ref-back-300-0)[\[283\]](#refnote-300)[&#8202;](#ref-back-GAU-22/A_resolved-2)[\[201\]](#refnote-GAU-22/A_resolved)

Operational test and evaluation (OT&E) with Block 3F, the final configuration for SDD, began in December 2018, but its completion was delayed particularly by technical problems in integration with the DOD's Joint Simulation Environment (JSE);[&#8202;](#ref-back-301-0)[\[284\]](#refnote-301) the F-35 finally completed all JSE trials in September 2023.[&#8202;](#ref-back-F-35MilestoneC-1)[\[76\]](#refnote-F-35MilestoneC)

### United States

#### Training

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a5/Navy_launches_first_local_F-35C_sortie_%28cropped%29.jpg/500px-Navy_launches_first_local_F-35C_sortie_%28cropped%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Navy_launches_first_local_F-35C_sortie_(cropped).jpg) The first F-35C Lightning II sortie takes off from [VFA-101](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VFA-101 "VFA-101") at [Eglin Air Force Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eglin_Air_Force_Base "Eglin Air Force Base")

The F-35A and F-35B were cleared for basic flight training in early 2012, despite concerns that the system's immaturity could reduce safety and performance.[&#8202;](#ref-back-302-0)[\[285\]](#refnote-302)[&#8202;](#ref-back-303-0)[\[286\]](#refnote-303)[&#8202;](#ref-back-304-0)[\[287\]](#refnote-304) During the Low Rate Initial Production (LRIP) phase, the three US military services jointly developed tactics and procedures using flight simulators, testing effectiveness, discovering problems, and refining design. On 10 September 2012, the USAF began an operational utility evaluation (OUE) of the F-35A, including logistical support, maintenance, personnel training, and pilot execution.[&#8202;](#ref-back-305-0)[\[288\]](#refnote-305)[&#8202;](#ref-back-C4WDefault-4)[\[289\]](#refnote-C4WDefault)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0a/F-35C_NAS_Fallon.jpg/500px-F-35C_NAS_Fallon.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:F-35C_NAS_Fallon.jpg) A pair of F-35Cs and [F/A-18E/Fs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boeing_F/A-18E/F_Super_Hornet "Boeing F/A-18E/F Super Hornet") fly over [NAS Fallon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NAS_Fallon "NAS Fallon"), home of [TOPGUN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TOPGUN "TOPGUN"), in September 2015.

The USMC F-35B [Fleet Replacement Squadron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fleet_Replacement_Squadron "Fleet Replacement Squadron") (FRS) was initially based at Eglin AFB in 2012 alongside USAF F-35A training units, before moving to [MCAS Beaufort](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MCAS_Beaufort "MCAS Beaufort") in 2014; another FRS was stood up at [MCAS Miramar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MCAS_Miramar "MCAS Miramar") in 2020.[&#8202;](#ref-back-307-0)[\[290\]](#refnote-307)[&#8202;](#ref-back-308-0)[\[291\]](#refnote-308) The USAF holds its basic F-35A course at Eglin AFB and Luke AFB; in January 2013, training began at Eglin with capacity for 100 pilots and 2,100 maintainers at once.[&#8202;](#ref-back-pilot_training-0)[\[292\]](#refnote-pilot_training) The [6th Weapons Squadron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/6th_Weapons_Squadron "6th Weapons Squadron") of the [USAF Weapons School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USAF_Weapons_School "USAF Weapons School") was activated at [Nellis AFB](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nellis_AFB "Nellis AFB") in June 2017 for F-35A weapons instructor curriculum; the [65th Aggressor Squadron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/65th_Aggressor_Squadron "65th Aggressor Squadron") was reactivated with the F-35A in June 2022 to expand training against adversary stealth aircraft tactics.[&#8202;](#ref-back-310-0)[\[293\]](#refnote-310) The USN stood up its F-35C FRS in 2012— [VFA-101](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VFA-101 "VFA-101") at Eglin AFB—then in 2019 consolidated operations under [VFA-125](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VFA-125 "VFA-125") at [NAS Lemoore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NAS_Lemoore "NAS Lemoore") in 2019.[&#8202;](#ref-back-311-0)[\[294\]](#refnote-311) The F-35C was introduced to the Strike Fighter Tactics Instructor course, or [TOPGUN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TOPGUN "TOPGUN"), in 2020, with the course syllabus revamped to accommodate the aircraft's additional capabilities.[&#8202;](#ref-back-312-0)[\[295\]](#refnote-312)

#### US Marine Corps

On 16 November 2012, the USMC received the first F-35B of [VMFA-121](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VMFA-121 "VMFA-121") at [MCAS Yuma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marine_Corps_Air_Station_Yuma "Marine Corps Air Station Yuma").[&#8202;](#ref-back-313-0)[\[296\]](#refnote-313) The USMC declared Initial Operational Capability (IOC) for the F-35B in the Block 2B configuration on 31 July 2015 after operational trials, with some limitations in night operations, mission systems, and weapons carriage.[&#8202;](#ref-back-USMC_IOC-3)[\[2\]](#refnote-USMC_IOC)[&#8202;](#ref-back-314-0)[\[297\]](#refnote-314) USMC F-35Bs participated in their first Red Flag exercise in July 2016 with 67 sorties conducted.[&#8202;](#ref-back-315-0)[\[298\]](#refnote-315) The first F-35B deployment occurred in 2017 at MCAS Iwakuni, Japan; combat employment began in July 2018 from the amphibious assault ship [USS _Essex_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS_Essex_(LHD-2) "USS Essex (LHD-2)"), with the first combat strike on 27 September 2018 against a [Taliban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taliban "Taliban") target in Afghanistan.[&#8202;](#ref-back-316-0)[\[299\]](#refnote-316)

During a conflict, the USMC plans to disperse the aircraft among austere forward-deployed bases with shelter and concealment to improve survivability while remaining close to a battlespace. Known as distributed STOVL operations (DSO), F-35Bs would operate from temporary bases in allied territory within hostile missile engagement zones and displace inside the enemy's 24- to 48-hour targeting cycle; this strategy allows F-35Bs to rapidly respond to operational needs, with mobile forward arming and refueling points (M-FARPs) accommodating [KC-130](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lockheed_Martin_KC-130 "Lockheed Martin KC-130") and [MV-22 Osprey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MV-22_Osprey "MV-22 Osprey") aircraft to rearm and refuel the jets, as well as littoral areas for sea links of mobile distribution sites. For higher echelons of maintenance, F-35Bs would return from M-FARPs to rear-area friendly bases or ships. Helicopter-portable metal planking is needed to protect unprepared roads from the F-35B's exhaust; the USMC is studying lighter heat-resistant options.[&#8202;](#ref-back-317-0)[\[300\]](#refnote-317) These operations have become part of the larger USMC Expeditionary Advanced Base Operations (EABO) concept.[&#8202;](#ref-back-318-0)[\[301\]](#refnote-318)

The first USMC F-35C squadron, [VMFA-314](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VMFA-314 "VMFA-314"), achieved [full operational capability](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Full_operational_capability "Full operational capability") in July 2021 and was first deployed on board USS _Abraham Lincoln_ as a part of Carrier Air Wing 9 in January 2022.[&#8202;](#ref-back-319-0)[\[302\]](#refnote-319)

In 2024, Lt. Gen. Sami Sadat of Afghanistan described an operation in which F-35Bs from [USS _Essex_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS_Essex_(LHD-2) "USS Essex (LHD-2)") bombed a Taliban position through cloud cover. "The impact \[&#8202;](#ref-back-320-0)[the F-35\] left on my soldiers was amazing. Like, whoa, you know, we have this technology", Sadat said. "But also the impact on the Taliban was quite crippling, because they have never seen Afghan forces move in the winter, and they have never seen planes that could bomb through the clouds."[\[303\]](#refnote-320)

On 9 November 2024, Marine F-35Cs struck targets associated with the [Houthi movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houthis "Houthis") in [Yemen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yemen "Yemen") during the [Red Sea crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Sea_crisis "Red Sea crisis")—possibly the first time the F-35C was used in combat.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Trevithickyemen-0)[\[304\]](#refnote-Trevithickyemen)

#### US Air Force

USAF F-35As first achieved IOC in 2016: aircraft in the Block 3i configuration with the [34th Fighter Squadron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/34th_Fighter_Squadron "34th Fighter Squadron") at [Hill Air Force Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hill_Air_Force_Base "Hill Air Force Base"), [Utah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Utah "Utah"), on 2 August.[&#8202;](#ref-back-defready_aug16-2)[\[3\]](#refnote-defready_aug16) F-35As conducted their first Red Flag exercise the following year; system maturity had improved and the aircraft scored a kill ratio of 15:1 against an F-16 [aggressor squadron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aggressor_squadron "Aggressor squadron") in a high-threat environment.[&#8202;](#ref-back-322-0)[\[305\]](#refnote-322) F-35As were first deployed on 15 April 2019 to [Al Dhafra Air Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Dhafra_Air_Base "Al Dhafra Air Base"), UAE;[&#8202;](#ref-back-323-0)[\[306\]](#refnote-323) 12 days later, they were first used in combat—in an airstrike on an [Islamic State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_State_of_Iraq_and_the_Levant "Islamic State of Iraq and the Levant") tunnel network in northern Iraq.[&#8202;](#ref-back-usaf20190427-0)[\[307\]](#refnote-usaf20190427)

F-35As were first stationed in Europe in 2021, when two squadrons totaling 48 aircraft were added to the [48th Fighter Wing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/48th_Fighter_Wing "48th Fighter Wing")'s [F-15C](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McDonnell_Douglas_F-15_Eagle "McDonnell Douglas F-15 Eagle") and [F-15E](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McDonnell_Douglas_F-15E_Strike_Eagle "McDonnell Douglas F-15E Strike Eagle") squadrons at [RAF Lakenheath](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RAF_Lakenheath "RAF Lakenheath") in the UK. The first aircraft of the 495th Fighter Squadron arrived on 15 December 2021.[&#8202;](#ref-back-325-0)[\[308\]](#refnote-325)[&#8202;](#ref-back-The_Aviationist-0)[\[309\]](#refnote-The_Aviationist)

The F-35's operating cost is higher than some older USAF tactical aircraft. In 2015, the cost per flight hour (CPFH) of the A-10 was $17,716; the F-15C, $41,921; and the F-16C, $22,514.[&#8202;](#ref-back-327-0)[\[310\]](#refnote-327) In fiscal year 2018, the F-35A's CPFH was $44,000; this shrank to $35,000 in 2019,[&#8202;](#ref-back-328-0)[\[311\]](#refnote-328) and Lockheed Martin said it hoped to reduce it to $25,000 by 2025 through performance-based logistics and other measures.[&#8202;](#ref-back-329-0)[\[312\]](#refnote-329)

In 2025, US officials said F-35As and [F-22s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lockheed_Martin_F-22_Raptor "Lockheed Martin F-22 Raptor") launched from land bases in the Persian Gulf region were used to attempt to draw surface-to-air missile fire ahead of [B-2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northrop_B-2_Spirit "Northrop B-2 Spirit") bombing runs during the [United States strikes on Iranian nuclear sites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_United_States_strikes_on_Iranian_nuclear_sites "2025 United States strikes on Iranian nuclear sites").[&#8202;](#ref-back-330-0)[\[313\]](#refnote-330)[&#8202;](#ref-back-331-0)[\[314\]](#refnote-331)[&#8202;](#ref-back-332-0)[\[315\]](#refnote-332)

In January 2026, F-35s participated in the [United States strikes in Venezuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_strikes_in_Venezuela "2026 United States strikes in Venezuela"), along with [F-22s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lockheed_Martin_F-22_Raptor "Lockheed Martin F-22 Raptor"), [B-1s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/B-1_Lancer "B-1 Lancer"), [F/A-18s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boeing_F/A-18E/F_Super_Hornet "Boeing F/A-18E/F Super Hornet"), [EA-18s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boeing_EA-18G_Growler "Boeing EA-18G Growler"), and various intelligence, surveillance, and reconnaissance aircraft and drones. General [Dan Caine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dan_Caine "Dan Caine") claimed the aircraft were used to disable Venezuelan air defense systems to ensure the safe passage of the helicopters into the target area. The operation resulted in the capture of Venezuelan President [Nicolás Maduro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicol%C3%A1s_Maduro "Nicolás Maduro").[&#8202;](#ref-back-333-0)[\[316\]](#refnote-333)

#### US Navy

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/56/VFA-147_F-35C_catches_the_wire_on_USS_George_Washington.jpg/500px-VFA-147_F-35C_catches_the_wire_on_USS_George_Washington.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:VFA-147_F-35C_catches_the_wire_on_USS_George_Washington.jpg) VFA-147 F-35C catches the wire on [USS _George Washington_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS_George_Washington_(CVN-73) "USS George Washington (CVN-73)")

The USN achieved operational status with the F-35C in Block 3F on 28 February 2019.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:0-3)[\[4\]](#refnote-:0) On 2 August 2021, the F-35C of [VFA-147](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VFA-147 "VFA-147"), as well as the [CMV-22 Osprey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bell_Boeing_V-22_Osprey "Bell Boeing V-22 Osprey"), embarked on their maiden deployments as part of Carrier Air Wing 2 on board [USS _Carl Vinson_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS_Carl_Vinson "USS Carl Vinson").[&#8202;](#ref-back-334-0)[\[317\]](#refnote-334)

USN F-35Cs operating from the USS _Carl Vinson_ took part in the training exercise Pacific Stellar 2025 in February, along with the French and Japanese navies.[&#8202;](#ref-back-335-0)[\[318\]](#refnote-335)

In April 2025, F-35Cs from VFA-97 shot down multiple Houthi drones over the Red Sea, making it the first time the Navy has used the variant in combat.[&#8202;](#ref-back-336-0)[\[319\]](#refnote-336)

### United Kingdom

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4d/F-35B_Lightning_II_lands_aboard_HMS_Queen_Elizabeth_%28R08%29_on_17_October_2019_%28191017-N-QI061-1159%29.jpg/500px-F-35B_Lightning_II_lands_aboard_HMS_Queen_Elizabeth_%28R08%29_on_17_October_2019_%28191017-N-QI061-1159%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:F-35B_Lightning_II_lands_aboard_HMS_Queen_Elizabeth_(R08)_on_17_October_2019_(191017-N-QI061-1159).jpg) F-35B _ZM148_ of [No. 617 Squadron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/No._617_Squadron_RAF "No. 617 Squadron RAF") landing on HMS _Queen Elizabeth_, 2019

The United Kingdom's [Royal Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Air_Force "Royal Air Force") and Royal Navy operate the F-35B. Called Lightning in British service,[&#8202;](#ref-back-337-0)[\[320\]](#refnote-337) it has replaced the [Harrier GR9](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Aerospace_Harrier_II "British Aerospace Harrier II"), retired in 2010, and [Tornado GR4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panavia_Tornado "Panavia Tornado"), retired in 2019. The F-35 is to be Britain's primary strike aircraft for the next three decades. One of the Royal Navy's requirements was a [Shipborne Rolling and Vertical Landing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SRVL "SRVL") (SRVL) mode to increase maximum landing weight by using wing lift during landing.[&#8202;](#ref-back-338-0)[\[321\]](#refnote-338)[&#8202;](#ref-back-339-0)[\[322\]](#refnote-339) Like the Italian Navy, British F-35Bs use [ski-jumps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ski-jump_ramp "Ski-jump ramp") to fly from their aircraft carriers, [HMS _Queen Elizabeth_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HMS_Queen_Elizabeth_(R08) "HMS Queen Elizabeth (R08)") and [HMS _Prince of Wales_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HMS_Prince_of_Wales_(R09) "HMS Prince of Wales (R09)"). British F-35Bs are not intended to use the Brimstone 2 missile.[&#8202;](#ref-back-340-0)[\[323\]](#refnote-340) In July 2013, [Chief of the Air Staff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chief_of_the_Air_Staff_(United_Kingdom) "Chief of the Air Staff (United Kingdom)") Air Chief Marshal [Sir Stephen Dalton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sir_Stephen_Dalton "Sir Stephen Dalton") announced that [No. 617 Squadron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/No._617_Squadron_RAF "No. 617 Squadron RAF") would be the RAF's first operational F-35 squadron.[&#8202;](#ref-back-at19j13-0)[\[324\]](#refnote-at19j13)[&#8202;](#ref-back-gov18j13-0)[\[325\]](#refnote-gov18j13)

The first British F-35 squadron was [No. 17 (Reserve) Test and Evaluation Squadron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/No._17_Squadron_RAF "No. 17 Squadron RAF") (TES), which stood up on 12 April 2013 as the aircraft's [Operational Evaluation Unit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operational_Evaluation_Unit "Operational Evaluation Unit").[&#8202;](#ref-back-343-0)[\[326\]](#refnote-343) By June 2013, the RAF had received three F-35s of the 48 on order, initially based at Eglin Air Force Base.[&#8202;](#ref-back-344-0)[\[327\]](#refnote-344) In June 2015, the F-35B undertook its first launch from a ski-jump at NAS Patuxent River.[&#8202;](#ref-back-345-0)[\[328\]](#refnote-345) On 5 July 2017, it was announced that the second UK-based RAF squadron would be [No. 207 Squadron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/No._207_Squadron_RAF "No. 207 Squadron RAF"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-346-0)[\[329\]](#refnote-346) which reformed on 1 August 2019 as the Lightning [Operational Conversion Unit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operational_Conversion_Unit "Operational Conversion Unit") (OCU).[&#8202;](#ref-back-347-0)[\[330\]](#refnote-347) No. 617 Squadron reformed on 18 April 2018 during a ceremony in Washington, D.C., becoming the first RAF front-line squadron to operate the type;[&#8202;](#ref-back-348-0)[\[331\]](#refnote-348) receiving its first four F-35Bs on 6 June, flying from MCAS Beaufort to [RAF Marham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RAF_Marham "RAF Marham").[&#8202;](#ref-back-349-0)[\[332\]](#refnote-349) On 10 January 2019, No. 617 Squadron and its F-35s were declared combat-ready.[&#8202;](#ref-back-350-0)[\[333\]](#refnote-350)

April 2019 saw the first overseas deployment of a UK F-35 squadron when No. 617 Squadron went to [RAF Akrotiri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RAF_Akrotiri "RAF Akrotiri"), [Cyprus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyprus "Cyprus").[&#8202;](#ref-back-351-0)[\[334\]](#refnote-351) This reportedly led on 25 June 2019 to the first combat use of an RAF F-35B: an armed reconnaissance flight searching for Islamic State targets in Iraq and Syria.[&#8202;](#ref-back-352-0)[\[335\]](#refnote-352) In October 2019, F-35s of 617 Squadron and No. 17 TES were embarked on HMS _Queen Elizabeth_ for the first time.[&#8202;](#ref-back-353-0)[\[336\]](#refnote-353) No. 617 Squadron departed RAF Marham on 22 January 2020 for their first [Exercise Red Flag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exercise_Red_Flag "Exercise Red Flag") with the Lightning.[&#8202;](#ref-back-354-0)[\[337\]](#refnote-354) As of November 2022, 26 F-35Bs were based in the United Kingdom (with 617 and 207 Squadrons) and a further three were permanently based in the United States (with 17 Squadron) for testing and evaluation purposes.[&#8202;](#ref-back-auto4-0)[\[338\]](#refnote-auto4)

The UK's second operational squadron is the [Fleet Air Arm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fleet_Air_Arm "Fleet Air Arm")'s [809 Naval Air Squadron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/809_Naval_Air_Squadron "809 Naval Air Squadron"), which stood up in December 2023.[&#8202;](#ref-back-356-0)[\[339\]](#refnote-356)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Royal_Navy-0)[\[340\]](#refnote-Royal_Navy)[&#8202;](#ref-back-358-0)[\[341\]](#refnote-358)

In June 2025, as part of its second phase of procurement, the UK announced plans to procure 12 F-35As, capable of delivering both conventional and nuclear weapons, including the [B61-12](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/B61_nuclear_bomb#B61_Mod_12 "B61 nuclear bomb") thermonuclear gravity bomb. The aircraft will form part of NATO's [dual-capable aircraft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_sharing#NATO "Nuclear sharing") program. The F-35As will be based in RAF Marham to be used in a training role on routine operations as part of the OCU. 15 F-35Bs are also planned in this phase.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:10-0)[\[342\]](#refnote-:10)[&#8202;](#ref-back-360-0)[\[343\]](#refnote-360)[&#8202;](#ref-back-:11-0)[\[344\]](#refnote-:11)

In July 2025, United States B61 nuclear weapons were redeployed to the US-operated [RAF Lakenheath](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RAF_Lakenheath "RAF Lakenheath") for the first time since 2008. The base operates F-35As and [F-15Es](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McDonnell_Douglas_F-15E_Strike_Eagle "McDonnell Douglas F-15E Strike Eagle"), both capable of delivering the B61.[&#8202;](#ref-back-y1942-0)[\[345\]](#refnote-y1942)[&#8202;](#ref-back-363-0)[\[346\]](#refnote-363)

On March 3, 2026, the Ministry of Defence confirmed that a British F-35B shot down a hostile drone over Jordan, marking the first time a British F-35 has destroyed a target in operations.[&#8202;](#ref-back-364-0)[\[347\]](#refnote-364)

### Australia

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/28/Four_No_3_Squadron_RAAF_F-35As_during_Talisman_Sabre_2023.jpg/500px-Four_No_3_Squadron_RAAF_F-35As_during_Talisman_Sabre_2023.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Four_No_3_Squadron_RAAF_F-35As_during_Talisman_Sabre_2023.jpg) Four F-35As assigned to [No. 3 Squadron RAAF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/No._3_Squadron_RAAF "No. 3 Squadron RAAF") in 2023

Australia's first F-35, designated A35-001, was manufactured in 2014, with flight training provided through the international Pilot Training Center (PTC) at Luke Air Force Base in Arizona.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:5-0)[\[348\]](#refnote-:5) The first two F-35s were unveiled to the Australian public on 3 March 2017 at the [Avalon Airshow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_International_Airshow "Australian International Airshow").[&#8202;](#ref-back-:6-0)[\[349\]](#refnote-:6) By 2021, the [Royal Australian Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Australian_Air_Force "Royal Australian Air Force") had accepted 26 F-35As, with nine in the US and 17 operating at No 3 Squadron and No 2 Operational Conversion Unit at [RAAF Base Williamtown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RAAF_Base_Williamtown "RAAF Base Williamtown").[&#8202;](#ref-back-:5-1)[\[348\]](#refnote-:5) With 41 trained RAAF pilots and 225 trained technicians for maintenance, the fleet was declared ready to deploy on operations.[&#8202;](#ref-back-367-0)[\[350\]](#refnote-367) It was originally expected that Australia would receive all 72 F-35s by 2023.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:6-1)[\[349\]](#refnote-:6) Its final nine aircraft, which were the TR-3 version, arrived in Australia in December 2024.[&#8202;](#ref-back-DefenceJSFDec2024-0)[\[351\]](#refnote-DefenceJSFDec2024)

### Israel

The Israeli Air Force (IAF) declared the F-35 operationally capable on 6 December 2017.[&#8202;](#ref-back-369-0)[\[352\]](#refnote-369) According to Kuwaiti newspaper _Al Jarida_, in July 2018, a test mission of at least three IAF F-35s flew to Iran's capital [Tehran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tehran "Tehran") and back to [Tel Aviv](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tel_Aviv "Tel Aviv"). While publicly unconfirmed, regional leaders acted on the report; Iran's supreme leader [Ali Khamenei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ali_Khamenei "Ali Khamenei") reportedly fired the air force chief and commander of Iran's [Revolutionary Guard Corps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Revolutionary_Guard_Corps_Aerospace_Force "Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps Aerospace Force") over the mission.[&#8202;](#ref-back-370-0)[\[353\]](#refnote-370)[&#8202;](#ref-back-371-0)[\[354\]](#refnote-371)

On 22 May 2018, IAF chief [Amikam Norkin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amikam_Norkin "Amikam Norkin") said that the service had employed its F-35Is in two attacks on two battle fronts. This was part of the [May 2018 Israel-Iran clashes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/May_2018_Israel-Iran_clashes "May 2018 Israel-Iran clashes"), where airstrikes were claimed to target Iranian positions surrounding [Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syria "Syria")'s capital of [Damascus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Damascus "Damascus") and the buffer [UNDOF Zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UNDOF_Zone "UNDOF Zone") of the [occupied Golan Heights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_occupation_of_the_Golan_Heights "Israeli occupation of the Golan Heights").[&#8202;](#ref-back-372-0)[\[355\]](#refnote-372) This marks the first combat operation of an F-35 by any country.[&#8202;](#ref-back-first_strike-1)[\[17\]](#refnote-first_strike)[&#8202;](#ref-back-373-0)[\[356\]](#refnote-373) Norkin said it had been flown "all over the Middle East", and showed photos of an F-35I flying over [Beirut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beirut "Beirut") in daylight.[&#8202;](#ref-back-374-0)[\[357\]](#refnote-374) In July 2019, Israel expanded its strikes against Iranian missile shipments; IAF F-35Is allegedly struck Iranian targets in Iraq twice.[&#8202;](#ref-back-375-0)[\[358\]](#refnote-375)

In November 2020, the IAF announced that a recent four-plane delivery included a unique F-35I [testbed aircraft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Testbed_aircraft "Testbed aircraft") to be used to test and integrate Israeli-produced weapons and electronic systems on later F-35s. It is the only testbed F-35 delivered to a non-US customer.[&#8202;](#ref-back-376-0)[\[359\]](#refnote-376)[&#8202;](#ref-back-IsraelF35i-0)[\[360\]](#refnote-IsraelF35i)

On 11 May 2021, eight IAF F-35Is took part in an attack on 150 targets in [Hamas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamas "Hamas")' rocket array, including 50 to 70 launch pits in the northern [Gaza Strip](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaza_Strip "Gaza Strip"), as part of [Operation Guardian of the Walls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Guardian_of_the_Walls "Operation Guardian of the Walls").[&#8202;](#ref-back-378-0)[\[361\]](#refnote-378) On 6 March 2022, the IDF stated that on 15 March 2021, F-35Is shot down two Iranian [drones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unmanned_aerial_vehicle "Unmanned aerial vehicle") carrying weapons to the Gaza Strip.[&#8202;](#ref-back-379-0)[\[362\]](#refnote-379) This was the first operational interception and downing carried out by the F-35. They were also used in the [Gaza war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaza_war "Gaza war").[&#8202;](#ref-back-380-0)[\[363\]](#refnote-380)[&#8202;](#ref-back-381-0)[\[364\]](#refnote-381)[&#8202;](#ref-back-382-0)[\[365\]](#refnote-382) On 2 November 2023, the IDF posted on social media that they used an F-35I to shoot down a [Houthi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houthi "Houthi") cruise missile over the Red Sea that was fired from Yemen during the Gaza war.[&#8202;](#ref-back-383-0)[\[366\]](#refnote-383)

F-35Is were used in airstrikes against Lebanon, including aerial refueling in the country's airspace, in August and September 2024, as part of the [Israel–Hezbollah conflict (2023–present)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israel%E2%80%93Hezbollah_conflict_(2023%E2%80%93present) "Israel–Hezbollah conflict (2023–present)").[&#8202;](#ref-back-384-0)[\[367\]](#refnote-384)[&#8202;](#ref-back-auto6-1)[\[164\]](#refnote-auto6) F-35Is were used in the [29 September 2024 Israeli attacks on Yemen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/29_September_2024_Israeli_attacks_on_Yemen "29 September 2024 Israeli attacks on Yemen").[&#8202;](#ref-back-385-0)[\[368\]](#refnote-385) F-35Is were also reportedly involved in the [October 2024 Israeli strikes on Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/October_2024_Israeli_strikes_on_Iran "October 2024 Israeli strikes on Iran").[&#8202;](#ref-back-386-0)[\[369\]](#refnote-386) The F-35 was also used during the [Twelve-Day War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twelve-Day_War "Twelve-Day War"), equipped with low-observable [conformal fuel tanks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conformal_fuel_tank "Conformal fuel tank") to extend their range and allow operations over Iranian soil without mid-air refueling.[&#8202;](#ref-back-auto2-0)[\[370\]](#refnote-auto2) Iranian state-run media claimed that Iran shot down several Israeli F-35 jets, though the IDF denied the claims.[&#8202;](#ref-back-388-0)[\[371\]](#refnote-388)[&#8202;](#ref-back-389-0)[\[372\]](#refnote-389)

From 28 February 2026, Israeli F-35s participated in [US-Israeli strikes on Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_Iran_war#Iran "2026 Iran war"), beginning the [2026 Iran war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_Iran_war "2026 Iran war"). About 200 IAF jets, including F-35Is and [F-15s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McDonnell_Douglas_F-15_Eagle "McDonnell Douglas F-15 Eagle"), struck about 500 targets. The US had also sent more F-35s to the region during [the prewar buildup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_military_buildup_in_the_Middle_East "2026 United States military buildup in the Middle East").[&#8202;](#ref-back-390-0)[\[373\]](#refnote-390)[&#8202;](#ref-back-391-0)[\[374\]](#refnote-391) On 4 March, the IDF announced its F-35I shot down an [Islamic Republic of Iran Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Republic_of_Iran_Air_Force "Islamic Republic of Iran Air Force") [Yakovlev Yak-130](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yakovlev_Yak-130 "Yakovlev Yak-130") combat trainer over Tehran; the first claimed air-to-air kill of a crewed aircraft by any F-35, and the first IAF air-to-air kill of a crewed aircraft since November 1985, when an F-15 downed a pair of Syrian MiG-23s over Lebanon. According to the IAF, the engagement was conducted at beyond visual range using long-range missiles, and was described as a "rapid response" rather than a close-range dogfight. The Yak-130 was reportedly flying a counter-drone mission over the Iranian capital at the time. Unverified footage appeared to show the Yak-130 going down in a mountainous area north of Tehran, with two ejections visible.[&#8202;](#ref-back-392-0)[\[375\]](#refnote-392)[&#8202;](#ref-back-393-0)[\[376\]](#refnote-393)[&#8202;](#ref-back-394-0)[\[377\]](#refnote-394)[&#8202;](#ref-back-395-0)[\[378\]](#refnote-395)

The Israeli Air Force claimed F-35I wartime [combat readiness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Combat_readiness "Combat readiness") rates of 90%, while a study by the [United States Government Accountability Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Government_Accountability_Office "United States Government Accountability Office") showed a decline in combat readiness of American F-35s, dropping from 67% in 2021 to 44% in 2025. The IAF has used the F-35 frequently during recent conflicts, including the [2026 Iran war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_Iran_war "2026 Iran war"), and it has used multiple tiers of IAF staff who maintain the aircraft.[&#8202;](#ref-back-396-0)[\[379\]](#refnote-396)

### Italy

Italy's F-35As were declared to have reached initial operational capability (IOC) on 30 November 2018. At the time, Italy had taken delivery of 10 F-35As and one F-35B, with 2 F-35As and the one F-35B being stationed in the US for training; the remaining 8 F-35As were stationed in Amendola.[&#8202;](#ref-back-397-0)[\[380\]](#refnote-397) [Italian Navy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_Navy "Italian Navy") F-35Bs have been operating from the Italian aircraft carrier [ITS _Cavour_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_aircraft_carrier_Cavour "Italian aircraft carrier Cavour"), where they have also conducted drills in the [Philippine Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_Sea "Philippine Sea") with the US in 2024.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:8-0)[\[381\]](#refnote-:8)[&#8202;](#ref-back-399-0)[\[382\]](#refnote-399) Construction in 2023 showed that [Ghedi Air Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghedi_Air_Base "Ghedi Air Base") is planning to host F-35As for Italian delivery of US B61 nuclear weapons under NATO [nuclear sharing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_sharing "Nuclear sharing").[&#8202;](#ref-back-400-0)[\[383\]](#refnote-400)

### Japan

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a9/Japan_air_self_defense_force_Lockheed_Martin_F-35A_using_afterburner_RJSM.jpg/500px-Japan_air_self_defense_force_Lockheed_Martin_F-35A_using_afterburner_RJSM.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Japan_air_self_defense_force_Lockheed_Martin_F-35A_using_afterburner_RJSM.jpg) F-35A in afterburner at JASDF [Misawa Air Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Misawa_Air_Base "Misawa Air Base")

Japan's F-35As were declared to have reached initial operational capability (IOC) on 29 March 2019. At the time Japan had taken delivery of 10 F-35As stationed in [Misawa Air Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Misawa_Air_Base "Misawa Air Base"). Japan plans to eventually acquire a total of 147 F-35s, which will include 42 F-35Bs. It plans to use the latter variant to equip Japan's [_Izumo_-class](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Izumo-class_multi-purpose_destroyer "Izumo-class multi-purpose destroyer") multi-purpose destroyers.[&#8202;](#ref-back-401-0)[\[384\]](#refnote-401)[&#8202;](#ref-back-402-0)[\[385\]](#refnote-402) On August 7, 2025, the first three Japanese F-35B's landed at [Nyutabaru Air Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nyutabaru_Air_Base "Nyutabaru Air Base"). Four more are to arrive by March 2026.[&#8202;](#ref-back-403-0)[\[386\]](#refnote-403)

On 9 April 2019, a JASDF F-35A (tail number 79-8705) attached to [Misawa Air Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Misawa_Air_Base "Misawa Air Base") crashed east of the [Aomori Prefecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aomori_Prefecture "Aomori Prefecture") during a training mission over the Pacific Ocean at an estimated speed of Mach 0.9.[&#8202;](#ref-back-spatial_disorientation-0)[\[387\]](#refnote-spatial_disorientation) Japan grounded its 12 F-35As during the investigation. The US Navy and [Japan Maritime Self-Defense Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japan_Maritime_Self-Defense_Force "Japan Maritime Self-Defense Force") searched for the missing aircraft and pilot, finding debris soon afterward[&#8202;](#ref-back-spatial_disorientation-1)[\[387\]](#refnote-spatial_disorientation) and recovered the pilot's remains in June.[&#8202;](#ref-back-405-0)[\[388\]](#refnote-405) The aircraft [flight recorder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flight_recorder "Flight recorder") was too damaged for any data to be retrieved.[&#8202;](#ref-back-spatial_disorientation-2)[\[387\]](#refnote-spatial_disorientation) Despite speculation that China or Russia might attempt to [salvage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salvage_diving "Salvage diving") the aircraft, the [Japanese Defense Ministry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_Defense_Ministry "Japanese Defense Ministry") reported no "reported activities" from either country.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:1-0)[\[389\]](#refnote-:1) The pilot had radioed his intention to abort the drill and was apparently conscious and responsive until 15 seconds before crashing, but he sent no [distress signal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Distress_signal "Distress signal") nor attempted any recovery maneuvers as he descended at a rapid rate. The accident report attributed the cause to the pilot's [spatial disorientation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spatial_disorientation "Spatial disorientation").[&#8202;](#ref-back-spatial_disorientation-3)[\[387\]](#refnote-spatial_disorientation) As of 2025, this is the only known fatal crash of any F-35 variant.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:20-0)[\[390\]](#refnote-:20)

### Norway

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1e/First_Norwegian_F-35_Lightning_II_at_Luke_Air_Force_Base.JPG/500px-First_Norwegian_F-35_Lightning_II_at_Luke_Air_Force_Base.JPG?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:First_Norwegian_F-35_Lightning_II_at_Luke_Air_Force_Base.JPG) First Norwegian F-35A Lightning II at Luke Air Force Base, Arizona

On 6 November 2019, Norway declared initial operational capability (IOC) for its fleet of 15 F-35As out of a planned 52 F-35As.[&#8202;](#ref-back-408-0)[\[391\]](#refnote-408) On 6 January 2022, Norway's F-35As replaced its older F-16A and B models for the NATO quick reaction alert mission in the high north.[&#8202;](#ref-back-409-0)[\[392\]](#refnote-409) In April 2025, the total number of F-35s delivered totaled 49 out of 52.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:2-0)[\[393\]](#refnote-:2)[&#8202;](#ref-back-411-0)[\[394\]](#refnote-411)

On 22 September 2023, two F-35As from the [Royal Norwegian Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Norwegian_Air_Force "Royal Norwegian Air Force") landed on a motorway near [Tervo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tervo "Tervo"), Finland, showing, for the first time, that F-35As can operate from paved roads. Unlike the F-35B, they cannot land vertically. The fighters were also refueled with their engines running. Major General Rolf Folland, Commander of the RNAF, said: "Fighter jets are vulnerable on the ground, so by being able to use small airfields – and now motorways – (this) increases our survivability in war".[&#8202;](#ref-back-412-0)[\[395\]](#refnote-412)

### Netherlands

On 27 December 2021, the Netherlands declared initial operational capability (IOC) for its fleet of 24 F-35As it has received to date from its order for 46 F-35As.[&#8202;](#ref-back-auto3-0)[\[396\]](#refnote-auto3) In 2022, the Netherlands announced it will order an additional six F-35s, totaling 52 aircraft ordered.[&#8202;](#ref-back-414-0)[\[397\]](#refnote-414)As of September2024[\[update\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Lockheed_Martin_F-35_Lightning_II&action=edit), 40 out of the 52 ordered have been delivered, and the Netherlands seeks to order another six jets to help completely phase out their F-16 fleet.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:2-1)[\[393\]](#refnote-:2) As of 2026, under [Dutch participation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Netherlands_and_weapons_of_mass_destruction "Netherlands and weapons of mass destruction") in NATO [nuclear sharing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_sharing "Nuclear sharing"), the F-35As at [Volkel Air Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volkel_Air_Base "Volkel Air Base") are assigned a nuclear mission, with US [B61 Mod 12 nuclear bombs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/B61_nuclear_bomb "B61 nuclear bomb") available to them,[&#8202;](#ref-back-fasUS2026-1)[\[18\]](#refnote-fasUS2026) numbering 10 to 15 as of 2023.[&#8202;](#ref-back-fasShare2023-1)[\[126\]](#refnote-fasShare2023)

## Variants

The F-35 was designed with three initial variants – the F-35A, a CTOL land-based version; the F-35B, a STOVL version capable of use either on land or on aircraft carriers; and the F-35C, a CATOBAR carrier-based version. Since then, there has been work on the design of nationally specific versions for Israel and Canada.

- [![The three main variants: CTOL for conventional take-off and landing, STOVL for short take-off and vertical-landing, and CV for carrier variant](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/2/22/F-35_A_B_C_Config.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail_unscaled)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:F-35_A_B_C_Config.png "The three main variants: CTOL for conventional take-off and landing, STOVL for short take-off and vertical-landing, and CV for carrier variant")

   The three main variants: [CTOL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CTOL "CTOL") for conventional take-off and landing, [STOVL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/STOVL "STOVL") for short take-off and vertical-landing, and CV for carrier variant

### F-35A

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d4/JASDF_F-35A_Lightning_II_prepares_to_receive_fuel_from_USAF_KC-135_Stratotanker_-_February_2025.jpg/500px-JASDF_F-35A_Lightning_II_prepares_to_receive_fuel_from_USAF_KC-135_Stratotanker_-_February_2025.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:JASDF_F-35A_Lightning_II_prepares_to_receive_fuel_from_USAF_KC-135_Stratotanker_-_February_2025.jpg) [JASDF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japan_Air_Self-Defense_Force "Japan Air Self-Defense Force") F-35A Lightning II prepares to receive fuel from a USAF [KC-135 Stratotanker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KC-135_Stratotanker "KC-135 Stratotanker") in February 2025.

The F-35A is the [conventional take-off and landing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conventional_take-off_and_landing "Conventional take-off and landing") (CTOL) variant intended for the USAF and other air forces. It is the smallest, lightest version and capable of 9 [g](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G-force "G-force"), the highest of all variants.

Although the F-35A currently conducts [aerial refueling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aerial_refueling "Aerial refueling") via the boom and receptacle method, the aircraft can be modified for [probe-and-drogue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aerial_refueling#Probe-and-drogue "Aerial refueling") refueling if needed by the customer.[&#8202;](#ref-back-415-0)[\[398\]](#refnote-415)[&#8202;](#ref-back-416-0)[\[399\]](#refnote-416) A [drag chute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drogue_parachute "Drogue parachute") pod can be installed on the F-35A, with the [Royal Norwegian Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Norwegian_Air_Force "Royal Norwegian Air Force") being the first operator to adopt it.[&#8202;](#ref-back-auto-0)[\[400\]](#refnote-auto) The F-35A has a [tailhook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tailhook "Tailhook") designed to stop the aircraft during emergency situations. Unlike the more robust unit of the carrier-based F-35C, the F-35A's tailhook is a single-use device.[&#8202;](#ref-back-418-0)[\[401\]](#refnote-418)

### F-35B

See also: [Joint Combat Aircraft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint_Combat_Aircraft "Joint Combat Aircraft")

The F-35B is the [short take-off and vertical landing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Short_take-off_and_vertical_landing "Short take-off and vertical landing") (STOVL) variant of the aircraft. Similar in size to the A variant, the B sacrifices about a third of the A variant's fuel volume to accommodate the shaft-driven lift fan (SDLF).[&#8202;](#ref-back-419-0)[\[402\]](#refnote-419)[&#8202;](#ref-back-From_Archive.org-0)[\[403\]](#refnote-From_Archive.org) This variant is limited to 7g. Unlike other variants, the F-35B has no landing hook. The "STOVL/HOOK" control instead engages conversion between normal and vertical flight.[&#8202;](#ref-back-guyReac-0)[\[404\]](#refnote-guyReac)[&#8202;](#ref-back-422-0)[\[405\]](#refnote-422) The F-35B is capable of Mach1.6 (1,960km/h; 1,220mph) and can perform vertical and/or short take-off and landing ( [V/STOL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/V/STOL "V/STOL")).[&#8202;](#ref-back-lift1-1)[\[231\]](#refnote-lift1)

- [![F-35B cutaway with lift fan](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/0/0a/Lockheed_Martin_F-35B_Lightning_II_cutaway_with_liftfan.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail_unscaled)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Lockheed_Martin_F-35B_Lightning_II_cutaway_with_liftfan.jpg "F-35B cutaway with lift fan")
